# Report: Status of Development — 2026-07-27
> Пометка 14.08.26: упоминания ветки `DpoImplementation` в этом файле — historical; ветка-канон = `codex/dpo-implementation` (см. 14.08.26WorkLeft-05-branch-reconciliation-codex-dpo-implementation.md).

## What this report is

This report records, finding by finding, what the DPO Legal Learning code actually
contains as of 2026-07-27, measured ONLY against the plan of record. It is written
so that a person with no prior context can read any single finding and understand it.

## Premise and scope

- **Audited code:** the repository worktree `C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation`,
  git branch `DpoImplementation`, commit `0f221ac89` (2026-07-26), working tree clean.
  Every code statement in this report refers to that exact commit.
- **Plan of record:** the requirement-defining `active-codex-execution-*` files in
  `C:\Projects\xiigen-planning\llm mvp\dpo legal build\` — specifically: the original
  task file, plan blocks 00 through 15, guides 01 through 05, the numbered policy and
  reference files 01 through 07, the master plan `ILEARNINGDYNAMICGRAPH-PLAN-v1`, and
  the two-phase teacher owner file `two-phase-local-remote-overlay`.
- **Status ledgers are NOT truth.** The coverage matrix, readiness simulations, pass
  ledgers, acceptance files, and the execution control board in the same folder
  describe a snapshot from around 2026-07-14, roughly 2,706 commits behind the audited
  code. This report may quote what they CLAIM, but never treats a claim as a fact
  about the code.
- **The parallel `codex-*` and `claude-*` author tracks are entirely out of scope.**

## Ground rules for every finding in this file

1. Every factual statement cites a file and line (`path:line`).
2. "The code does X" and "a document claims X" are always separate sentences —
   a claim is never reported as a code fact.
3. Machine identifiers are always followed by a plain-language explanation the first
   time they appear in a finding.
4. Findings are appended to the END of this file, in the order they are produced.
   Existing content is never edited or reordered.

### Finding 1 — The three lower graph levels: node, orchestrator, hierarchy (Cycle 1, Task 1)

**What was checked.** Whether the plan's three lower graph levels actually exist in the audited code and connect: the graph NODE (a "learning unit" — the small matrix that turns many inputs into many candidate outputs for one domain), the ORCHESTRATOR (the async layer that drives units and flows to turn one request into one answer), and the LEVEL HIERARCHY (the five stacked levels, with execution paths tagged by a level label). Audited code: repository C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, commit 0f221ac89, working tree clean; no build was run; read/grep only.

**Context about the plan documents (a document claim, not a code fact).** The three plan blocks I measured against were written earlier and, throughout, describe these graph types as "NEW / ПРОЕКТ / в коде НЕТ / ОТСУТСТВУЕТ" (NEW / design-only / not in code / absent). For instance, the node block says the graph-node wrapper is a project that does not exist in code — "Статус: ПРОЕКТ (в коде нет)" (active-codex-execution-plan-01-node.md:447) — and the hierarchy block lists the node, edge and topology types as "ОТСУТСТВУЕТ" / absent (active-codex-execution-plan-05-partA-hierarchy.md:548-550). Those are claims about an older snapshot; the report's own premise notes the ledgers are roughly 2,706 commits behind the audited code. The code has since moved well past those claims. Every statement below is a fact read directly from the audited commit.

**R-01 — the node.** Document claim: the node requirement is "Узел графа: ILearningUnit ≡ ILogicalUnit (L2) — «упрощённая матрица»", i.e. the node is the existing logical-unit level wrapped as a graph node (active-codex-execution-plan-01-node.md:24). Code fact: the base unit contract ILogicalUnit<TInput,TOutput> (the smallest executable "cell" — a many-inputs-to-many-outputs unit) exists at 00.Core/LogicalContracts.cs:102. The graph-node layer on top of it also exists: the interface IDynamicGraphNode and its typed form IDynamicGraphNode<TInput,TOutput> are declared at 00.Core/DynamicGraphContracts.cs:12 and :22, the typed form's ExecuteAsync returning the many-to-many surface LogicalUnitResult<IQueryable<GraphNodeCandidate>> (DynamicGraphContracts.cs:25). The ten-field node "passport" DynamicGraphNodeDescriptor is at DynamicGraphContracts.cs:31 and one output candidate GraphNodeCandidate at :44. A concrete wrapper class, LogicalUnitDynamicGraphNode<TInput,TOutput>, exists at 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/LogicalUnitDynamicGraphNode.cs:11; its ExecuteAsync (line 109) really calls the wrapped unit's ExecuteAsync (line 134) and projects that typed result once into the queryable candidate shape, and it answers "not my domain" honestly rather than fabricating a result (lines 124-129). This is a working wrapper, not an empty stub.

**R-03 — the orchestrator.** Document claim: the orchestrator requirement is "Оркестратор: ILearningOrchestrator ≡ ILogicalOrchestrator (async, L4)", the async layer that drives the graph (active-codex-execution-plan-03-orchestrator.md:35). Code fact: the L4 contract ILogicalOrchestrator (the assistant-level coordinator over flows) exists at 00.Core/LogicalContracts.cs:211. The audited branch has a real dynamic-graph orchestration stack the plan did not yet know about. DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorRuntime (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorRuntime.cs:18) owns topology projection and compare-and-swap publishing while exposing a read-only topology source. DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorComposition (…/DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorComposition.cs:28) wires a concrete node into an invocation via CreateAxisSweepInvocation (line 119) — this is the composition that connects the orchestrator level to the node level. DynamicGraphCandidateInvocationOrchestrator (…/DynamicGraphCandidateInvocationOrchestrator.cs:12) actually drives a run: its RunAsync (line 168) reads and validates the topology snapshot (lines 292-352), checks the configured source node is present exactly once (lines 356-365), runs the candidate runner that invokes the node (line 394), and hands the produced node outputs to the assistant (line 523). The assistant LogicalOrchestratorAssistant (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:28) holds a real AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator (constructed at lines 423/432) and delegates to it: RunPreparedAsync calls _orchestrator.RunAsync (line 631) and RunPreparedDynamicGraphAsync calls adaptiveOrchestrator.RunWithInvocationNodeOutputsAsync (line 669). AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator then executes the per-edge L3 flow (its own comments at 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator.cs:361 and :1491 mark "Execute … L3 edge"). So the orchestrator genuinely drives the node → runner → candidates → flow chain; it is composed down to the node level (R-03 connects to R-01) and is not a shell.

**R-05 — the five-level hierarchy and its labels.** Document claim: the hierarchy block requires the five levels stacked with "помеченные цепочки исполнения" (labeled execution chains) (active-codex-execution-plan-05-partA-hierarchy.md:24). Code fact: the level labels are real, explicit strings in code — not merely an implied structure. The tokens "L2","L3","L4","L5" occur 210 times across 55 files. They are load-bearing, not just prose: the edge contract carries a data field IDynamicGraphEdge.AuthorityUnitLevel (00.Core/DynamicGraphContracts.cs:109-110), and DynamicGraphValidation actively REJECTS any edge whose AuthorityUnitLevel is not "L2" (00.Core/DynamicGraphValidation.cs:255-256, error code DYNAMIC_GRAPH_EDGE_AUTHORITY_LEVEL_INVALID). RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver hard-codes ExpectedLevel = "L2" (21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver.cs:14 and :42), and DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityExecutionPipeline hard-codes ExpectedAuthorityLevel = "L2" (…/DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityExecutionPipeline.cs:18). Whole types, probes and tests are named by level: DynamicGraphUntrainedHonestyL2Probe, DynamicGraphRegistryL3SelectedTargetExecutor, DynamicGraphL4TopologyConsumerTests, and the L5 marker in DynamicGraphMachine.cs:11 ("Marker type for the L5 dynamic-graph learning-machine boundary"); LearningMachineContracts.cs:199 notes the machine "sits above ILogicalOrchestrator in the hierarchy." One precision note (code fact): the four operational levels L2, L3, L4, L5 are explicitly labeled and enforced, but the literal token "L1" (the plan's top Personal/Global machine-pair level) appears nowhere in the code — a grep for "L1" in .cs files returns no matches. So the hierarchy is labeled and traceable for its lower four levels; the top pair level exists only as a type (ILearningMachinePair), without an "L1" string label.

**Verdict:** R-01 node = implemented (contract at 00.Core/DynamicGraphContracts.cs:22; working wrapper at 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/LogicalUnitDynamicGraphNode.cs:109); R-03 orchestrator = implemented and really drives units/flows, not a shell (contract at 00.Core/LogicalContracts.cs:211; driven via 15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphCandidateInvocationOrchestrator.cs:168 and LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:631/669); R-05 hierarchy = implemented and LABELED in code (not only implicit), enforced for levels L2–L5 at 00.Core/DynamicGraphValidation.cs:255-256, with the one caveat that the literal "L1" label is absent from code. Cross-cutting note: the plan blocks 01/03/05 still describe these three levels as design-only / not-in-code, so the plan text is stale relative to the audited commit, where all three are actually present and composed.

### Finding 2 — Is the edge authority trainable or hard-coded? (Cycle 1, Task 2)

**What was checked.** In this graph an EDGE (the connection between two learning units) is meant to carry an "authority" — a trust weight that decides how much to rely on that edge, i.e. on a fork, which downstream unit to hand the work to and with what confidence. The plan's core demand (R-02) is that this authority be LEARNED (produced by training), and (R-12) that it REPLACE nine hard-coded decision mechanisms in the runtime. The question: does any edge-authority value in the audited code actually originate from TRAINING (a trained artifact / learned parameter), or is every current authority deterministic (a constant, config, or hard-coded rule)? And can the "nine mechanisms" be identified in the code? Audited code: repository C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, commit 0f221ac89, working tree clean; no build was run; read/grep only.

**What the plan demands (document claims, not code facts).** Plan block 02 titles the requirement "Ребро графа ... + ОБУЧАЕМАЯ авторитетность ребра" — the edge plus a TRAINABLE edge authority (active-codex-execution-plan-02-edge.md:24). It designs the learned final authority as a NEW class `EdgeAuthorityUnit` and explicitly marks it "NEW (СКЕЛЕТ, цель PASS_AS_TRAINED_FEATURE)" — a skeleton whose GOAL is to become a trained feature (active-codex-execution-plan-02-edge.md:545). Plan block 12 titles its requirement "Замена скрытого детерминизма (классификация 9 механизмов + cutover на обучаемый авторитет ребра)" — replace hidden determinism by classifying 9 mechanisms and cutting over to the learned authority (active-codex-execution-plan-12-no-hidden-determinism.md:36). Both plan files also state their own status in their own words: "dpo_status: NOT_STARTED" and "dpo_priority: LAST" (active-codex-execution-plan-02-edge.md:5-6; active-codex-execution-plan-12-no-hidden-determinism.md:5-6). So by the plan's own account, the training stage has not begun. Those are claims; every statement below is read from the audited commit.

**Code fact — the authority machinery is real and is built to DEMAND a learned unit.** The edge-authority contract, resolver, execution pipeline, and guards all exist and are substantial. The shared "leakage-signal vocabulary" (the fixed list of six signals — candidate position, source path, source id, file order, correlation id, exact prompt — that the authority is forbidden to look at, so it cannot cheat by memorising position instead of meaning) is a real owned type (00.Core/DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityLeakageSignalVocabulary.cs:9-19). The resolver that picks the authority (RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver.cs) fails closed against determinism: it refuses any edge that claims deterministic final authority ("Learned edge authority cannot claim deterministic final authority", RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver.cs:44-45) and REQUIRES the resolved unit to be flagged learned ("Resolved edge authority unit is not learned", error EDGE_AUTHORITY_UNIT_NOT_LEARNED, RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver.cs:81-82). The pipeline then requires the returned decision to be stamped with the source label "trained_edge_unit" (DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityExecutionPipeline.cs:19, enforced at :1173). The topology validator likewise rejects any edge marked as a deterministic final authority (DynamicGraphValidation.cs:259-260). In short, the SCAFFOLD is honest: if you hand it a non-learned or deterministic unit, it says no.

**Code fact — but nothing trained actually exists to fill that scaffold; the label "trained_edge_unit" is just a string.** The role name "trained_edge_unit" that the pipeline demands is a plain string constant, not a checkpoint or a learned parameter (RoutingAuthorityVocabulary.cs:15 defines `TrainedEdgeUnitRole = "trained_edge_unit"`). Nothing loads a trained checkpoint to produce the authority. Decisively, NO production class implements the authority-deciding unit `ILogicalUnit<EdgeAuthorityInput, EdgeAuthorityDecision>` (the object that would actually rank the fork targets): the only four implementations in the whole repository live in the Tests directory (test doubles) — RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolverTests.cs:338, DynamicGraphL4TopologyConsumerTests.cs:1136, DynamicGraphEdgeCandidateOrderLeakageGuardUnitTests.cs:1555, DynamicGraphCandidateInvocationOrchestratorTests.cs:496. The plan's named learned classes `EdgeAuthorityUnit` and `SemanticEdgeRouterUnit` do not exist anywhere in the code (grep for either returns no production file). The single place in non-test code that registers a unit as learned (`isLearned: true`) is the capability trainer (DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:334-341, again at :814), and it registers a `Register<string, string>` generated-capability unit — a text-to-text unit with role "generated_capability" — NOT an edge-authority unit. So even the one production "training" registration does not produce an edge authority.

**Code fact — the operative runtime today is deterministic / has no learned edge authority at all.** In the default runtime wiring (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.CreateDefaultCoreImplementation) the only units registered are two deterministic ones, both with `isLearned: false`: the prompt compiler (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:372-379) and the domain classifier (:383-390). The learned-edge-authority "preload" (a set of already-trained units a trusted host could inject) is optional and is null on the default path — the block that would load them only runs `if (edgeAuthorityPreload is not null)` (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:395), and the two default builders pass null. The inputs feeding the authority are produced deterministically: the input materialiser builds its semantic tokens by splitting document text on separators (DeterministicDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityInputMaterializer.cs:14, tokeniser at :211-241), and the candidate generator is the deterministic baseline (DeterministicRegistryEdgeCandidateProvider.cs:14, role "baseline_candidate_generator"). Where AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator stamps a routing decision with the source "trained_edge_unit" (AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator.cs:2291,2299), that is a LABEL applied to whatever the pipeline returns; because no learned unit is registered, the resolver fails closed and that learned path can only run against a test double, never a trained artifact. Independent of Task 3 (which separately confirms the top-level machine cannot train), the authority's source here is therefore: deterministic baseline, or — in tests only — an emulated learned unit. No edge-authority value in the audited code originates from training.

**Code fact — R-12: the nine mechanisms are named individually in code but classified only in the plan; no cutover has happened.** The specific deterministic mechanisms the plan wants replaced do exist as real code: the multi-router MultiSubPhaseRouterUnit (21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/MultiSubPhaseRouterUnit.cs), the keyword unit scorer KeywordUnitScorer (26.TrainableRouter/Training/KeywordUnitScorer.cs:11), and the scenario phase planner OrchestrationScenarioPhasePlanner (26.TrainableRouter/Training/OrchestrationScenarioPhasePlanner.cs:10). So they can be pointed at one by one. But there is NO code artifact that enumerates or classifies "the nine" as a set: a search for "nine", "no-hidden-determinism", "classified_mechanism", or a mechanism-count list returns no such classification in code. That enumeration exists only as a table in the plan document (active-codex-execution-plan-12-no-hidden-determinism.md:444-454). The cutover itself — replacing those deterministic finals with a learned authority — has not occurred, because no learned final authority exists in production; the deterministic mechanisms remain the operative decision-makers. The anti-determinism boundary is enforced structurally (the resolver and validator forbid a deterministic final authority), but that enforcement is vacuous for the actual provenance of authority values, since there is no trained unit for it to protect. (Plan-vs-code drift, a code fact: the plan cites MultiSubPhaseRouterUnit.cs:283-284 with fixed weights 0.55/0.25/0.20/0.02; at the audited commit those exact weights are not at that line — the file has since changed — but the router is still deterministic.)

**Plain answer to the exact question.** Today the edge authority is DETERMINISTIC (or, on the new dynamic-graph path, simply absent at runtime) — it is NOT learned. No edge-authority value comes from a trained artifact or learned parameter; the learned-authority contract, resolver, pipeline and guards are a fully-built but UNFILLED scaffold that correctly refuses non-learned units, while the only thing that could fill it (a trained `EdgeAuthorityUnit`) exists nowhere outside test doubles. The nine mechanisms are identifiable individually in the code but are classified as a set only in the plan document, and the cutover to a learned authority has not been performed.

**Verdict:** R-02 edge authority (trainable) = PARTIAL — honest untrained scaffold: the trainable-authority contract and fail-closed pipeline are implemented (RegistryDynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityResolver.cs:81-82; DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityExecutionPipeline.cs:19), but the LEARNED authority itself is MISSING — no trained artifact, no production implementation of ILogicalUnit<EdgeAuthorityInput,EdgeAuthorityDecision> (only test doubles), and the runtime is deterministic (DeterministicRegistryEdgeCandidateProvider.cs:14; RoutingAuthorityVocabulary.cs:15 shows "trained_edge_unit" is only a string label). R-12 hidden-determinism cutover = CLAIMED-ONLY / not done — the nine-mechanism classification lives only in the plan (active-codex-execution-plan-12-no-hidden-determinism.md:444-454); individual mechanisms exist in code (KeywordUnitScorer.cs:11, OrchestrationScenarioPhasePlanner.cs:10) but no learned authority has replaced them, so the deterministic mechanisms remain operative (default wiring registers only isLearned:false units, LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:372-390).

### Finding 3 — The top-level graph machine: fail-closed? And do the checkpoint carriers exist? (Cycle 1, Task 3)

**What was checked.** The top level of the graph is the "graph machine" — the object that would make the whole graph trainable, able to train, evaluate, export, and import like any model. Two questions: (a) does EVERY training-lifecycle entry point of this machine fail closed (honestly return "not activated"), with no hidden path that actually trains, fakes a result, or loads a real checkpoint; and (b) do the plan's two "carrier" records exist anywhere in code — `GraphCheckpointManifest` (a "checkpoint passport": a small record that describes and points to a saved trained result so it can be moved around safely) and `GraphLearningExport` (a "portable export": the package that carries a trained result out of the machine and into another one)? Audited code: repository C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, commit 0f221ac89, working tree clean; no build was run; read/grep only.

**What the plan demands (document claims, not code facts).** The plan calls for the graph top level to be a trainable machine (active-codex-execution-plan-04-dynamicgraph-machine.md:24). The plan requires the two carrier records plus promotion gates around them (active-codex-execution-plan-10-training-lifecycle.md:22-23). The plan warns that the restore order must never let a metadata import stand in for loading real trained weights (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:10). Those are claims about what SHOULD exist; every statement below is read directly from the audited commit.

**Code fact — the machine's four training-lifecycle entry points all fail closed, and there is no fifth hidden path.** The graph machine is a single sealed class `DynamicGraphMachine` (the one and only implementation of the graph learning-machine interface) at 15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphMachine.cs:22. It advertises its own honesty as compile-time constants: `RuntimeMode = "honest_untrained"` (a plain flag meaning "this level is a scaffold, not a trained model") at :25, and `SemanticQualityClaimed = false` (it claims no real quality) at :26. Its four lifecycle methods each return a failure result and never a success. `TrainBatchAsync` (one incremental training step) returns the error code DYNAMIC_GRAPH_TRAINING_NOT_ACTIVATED at :45-52. `EvaluateAsync` (measure accuracy on held-out data) returns DYNAMIC_GRAPH_EVALUATION_NOT_ACTIVATED at :54-61. `ExportAsync` (produce a portable trained package) returns DYNAMIC_GRAPH_EXPORT_NOT_ACTIVATED at :63-68. `ImportAsync` (load a package into the machine) returns DYNAMIC_GRAPH_IMPORT_NOT_ACTIVATED at :70-77. None of these four mutates any weight, writes a checkpoint, or reads one; each simply returns "not activated" (or the shared "CANCELLED" result if the caller cancelled first). The fifth method, `GetOrchestratorAsync` (:79-136), is NOT a training method — the interface itself describes it as returning the runtime coordinator, not a trained artifact (00.Core/LearningMachineContracts.cs:235-238). In this scaffold it only calls the injected delegate `_orchestratorFactory` to build the pre-existing baseline orchestrator, validates that result, and returns it unchanged (:88, :99, :125-135); it does not train, mutate weights, or load/return a checkpoint. The class is `sealed` and has exactly these five public methods plus a constructor — there are no private helper methods and no other branch that trains. A grep of the whole repository for OTHER implementations of the graph learning-machine interface `ILearningMachine<DynamicGraphDomain>` (the top-level trainable-machine contract, specialised to the graph domain) returns only this class (DynamicGraphMachine.cs:22) and one test assertion (Tests/DynamicGraphMachineHonestUntrainedTests.cs:28) — no alternate class exists anywhere that could bypass the scaffold. The honesty is proven by tests: the honest-untrained suite asserts all four lifecycle methods fail with their method-specific NOT_ACTIVATED codes WITHOUT the factory ever being called (a `factoryCalls` counter stays 0), even with null inputs and under a pre-cancelled token (DynamicGraphMachineHonestUntrainedTests.cs:41-98).

**Code fact — neither of the two required carrier records exists, under any spelling.** A repository-wide grep for the exact names `GraphCheckpointManifest` and `GraphLearningExport` returns ZERO hits — no code, no test, no fixture. A grep for the looser spellings `GraphCheckpoint` and `GraphExport` also returns ZERO hits. The looser term `CheckpointManifest` does return many hits, but every one belongs to a DIFFERENT trainable domain, never the graph: `GeneratedCapabilityCheckpointManifest` in the capability-training area (18.CapabilityTraining/TeachingRegistrySnapshot.cs:46), `SkillSelectionCheckpointManifest` in the smart-skills area (43.SmartSkillsDomain/SkillSelectionContracts.cs:651), `PromptToTaskCheckpointManifest` (43.SmartSkillsDomain/PromptToTaskMachine.cs:49), `OrchestrationReasoningCheckpointManifest` (43.SmartSkillsDomain/OrchestrationReasoningMachine.cs:55), and a file-name constant `CheckpointManifestFileName = "checkpoint-manifest.json"` in the design-conversion area (44.DesignConversionDomain/DesignConversionRealS3EvidenceBundleValidator.cs:61). Each of these is a name-alike for another domain's own passport, not the graph's; none of them is the graph carrier `GraphCheckpointManifest`, and there is no `...LearningExport` type for the graph either.

**Code fact — what exists generically, and the delta to the two graph-specific carriers.** One generic export record does exist and is shared by every domain: `LearningMachineExport` (the portable transfer record for a trained checkpoint) at 00.Core/LearningMachineContracts.cs:117-132, with the fields MachineId, DomainTag, Version, CheckpointRef (the storage key for the model weights), ManifestRef (the storage key for the unit manifest), AccuracyAtExport, and ExportedAtUtc. This one generic record is the declared RETURN type of the graph machine's `ExportAsync` and the declared INPUT type of its `ImportAsync` (DynamicGraphMachine.cs:63 and :70) — but the graph machine never actually produces or consumes one, because both methods fail closed. The delta, in plain words: what exists is (1) a single domain-agnostic export DTO (`LearningMachineExport`) and (2) each OTHER trainable domain's own manifest record. What the plan requires for the graph specifically — a `GraphCheckpointManifest` (the graph's own checkpoint passport) and a `GraphLearningExport` (the graph's own portable export) — does not exist at all; the graph domain has no dedicated carrier of either kind, no graph-specific manifest carrying graph weights, and the shared export DTO is never filled for the graph. On the plan's restore-order warning (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:10): because `ImportAsync` is inert (it just returns "not activated"), it cannot substitute a metadata import for loading real weights — but only because it loads nothing whatsoever, and the graph-specific carriers that would ever hold real weights do not exist.

**Plain answer to the exact question.** (a) Yes — the machine is genuinely fail-closed. Every one of its four training-lifecycle entry points returns a method-specific "not activated" failure; no weight is mutated and no checkpoint is written or read; the only non-training method just returns the pre-existing baseline coordinator; the class is the sole implementation of its interface; and tests prove the four methods fail without ever touching the factory. There is no hidden path that trains, fakes a result, or loads a real checkpoint. (b) No — neither carrier exists. `GraphCheckpointManifest` and `GraphLearningExport` appear nowhere in the code under any spelling; the only related types are one generic `LearningMachineExport` DTO and other domains' name-alike manifest records.

**Verdict:** R-04 graph machine fail-closed = IMPLEMENTED (honest untrained scaffold; all four lifecycle entry points fail closed at DynamicGraphMachine.cs:45-77; GetOrchestratorAsync merely returns the baseline coordinator at :79-136; sole implementation of ILearningMachine<DynamicGraphDomain> confirmed; honesty proven by Tests/DynamicGraphMachineHonestUntrainedTests.cs:41-98 — no hidden training path). R-10 lifecycle + carriers = MISSING for the carriers (both `GraphCheckpointManifest` and `GraphLearningExport` absent under any spelling; only a generic `LearningMachineExport` at 00.Core/LearningMachineContracts.cs:117-132 and other-domain name-alikes exist), with the graph training lifecycle present only as fail-closed stubs (DynamicGraphMachine.cs:45-77).

### Finding 4 — Async combinations: three caps and six behaviors (Cycle 1, Task 4)

**What the plan document promises (document-claim).** The planning document `active-codex-execution-plan-06-async-combinations.md` (lines 22-24) says one input must be run through several DIFFERENT combinations of learning units at the same time (a "fan-out"), then gathered back into one result (a "fan-in"). It names three separate limits that must exist and must mean different things: `MaxCombinations` (how many combination-plans are built for one input), `MaxParallelCombinationRuns` (how many of those plan-runs may execute at the same time), and `MaxParallelBranches` (how many branches may run at the same time INSIDE a single traversal). The same lines require deterministic ordering of results, cancellation, timeout, partial-failure handling, backpressure, and bounded concurrency. Those are claims in a document; the rest of this finding checks what the code actually does.

**Where the three limits are declared (code fact).** All three limits are fields on one small settings record named `AsyncCombinationBudget` — the "budget" attached to each run request — declared in `00.Core/DynamicGraphContracts.cs`: `MaxCombinations` at line 559, `MaxParallelBranches` at line 561, and `MaxParallelCombinationRuns` at line 562 (its default value is 1). So all three names genuinely exist in the code, exactly as the document names them. The question below is whether each is actually READ and used to throttle something, or merely declared.

**Cap 1 — `MaxCombinations` is consumed, but as a ceiling rather than the direct counter (code fact).** The number of combination-plans actually built for one input is driven by a separate field, `CombinationCount` (the caller's requested number). `AxisSweepCombinationStrategy` — the class that BUILDS the list of plans — stops enumerating as soon as it has produced `CombinationCount` plans (`21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:89`). `MaxCombinations` is consumed as the hard ceiling that `CombinationCount` may not exceed: it is read and enforced in the strategy's validation (`AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:134-135`), in the multi-combination runner's validation (`21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:508-509`), and in the single-combination runner's validation (`21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:391-392`). The multi-combination runner additionally rejects a plan list longer than `CombinationCount` (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:427`). So the count of built plans is bounded, and `MaxCombinations` is the enforced upper bound on it — consumed, though one level removed from the actual loop counter.

**Cap 2 — `MaxParallelCombinationRuns` is consumed as a real throttle (code fact).** In the multi-combination runner this cap directly limits how many plan-runs are in flight at once: `var parallelism = Math.Min(requestSnapshot.Budget.MaxParallelCombinationRuns, plans.Count)` (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135`); the runner seeds at most `parallelism` tasks (`:144-148`) and starts the next plan only as an earlier one finishes (`:162-169`). This is genuine bounded concurrency, not a declared-but-ignored field. A test proves it at runtime: with four plans and a cap of two, the node's observed maximum concurrency is exactly two (`Tests/BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunnerTests.cs:95`). The single-combination runner enforces this cap to be exactly 1 on its one-plan path (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:384`).

**Cap 3 — `MaxParallelBranches` is declared and validated but NOT consumed as a throttle (code fact).** This is the one real gap. Across all three files the cap is only checked to be at least 1 (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:393`, `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:510`, `AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:136`), copied into request snapshots (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:446`, `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:539`), and forwarded unchanged into each child plan's request (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:220`). Nothing reads it to limit concurrent branches — because these runners have no in-traversal branch fan-out to limit. The single-combination runner requires a plan of exactly one node (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:340`), resolves that one node (`:83`), and calls its `ExecuteAsync` exactly once (`:152`). A "traversal" here is a single node execution, so there are no sibling branches to run concurrently. (A same-named `MaxParallelBranches` IS actually enforced elsewhere — `21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/DeterministicDynamicGraphTopologyBuilder.cs:142` and `21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/InMemoryDynamicGraphTopologyRuntime.cs:487` — but those belong to a DIFFERENT settings record named `TopologyBuildBudget` (`00.Core/DynamicGraphTopologyBuildContracts.cs:32`) and check the shape of a graph at build time, not the runtime branch concurrency of an async combination run. They are name-alikes, not consumers of the async cap.)

**The six required behaviors (code fact).** Five of the six are implemented and, in the multi-combination runner, verified by tests. Deterministic ordering: plans are numbered in a fixed enumeration order (`AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:82-83`), each result is written into a fixed slot by that number (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:160`), and results are gathered back sorted by that number (`:363-371`); a test confirms results come back in plan order even when the last plan finishes first (`Tests/BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunnerTests.cs:80-82`). Cancellation and timeout: both flow through one linked cancellation scope that combines the caller's token, the request's own token, and an optional timeout timer, returning a `CANCELLED` or `TIMEOUT` status accordingly (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:129,525-526`; `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:113,136-141,172-181,649-650`), and tests exercise both (`Tests/BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunnerTests.cs:446` for cancel, `:488` for timeout). Partial-failure: in the multi-combination runner one failing plan does not abort the batch — the failure is captured per plan (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:235-240`), surviving candidates are still returned, and the batch is marked `PARTIAL_FAILURE`; only when every plan fails is an all-failed error returned (`:374-392`). Backpressure and bounded concurrency: both are delivered by the same admit-as-you-complete scheduling loop that Cap 2 drives (`BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135,144-148,162-169`). One honesty note: this "backpressure" is a plan-admission throttle only — there is no bounded Channel or SemaphoreSlim streaming buffer, and the optional typed result stream is always left null (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:306`, `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:413`). The sixth behavior — branch-level concurrency inside one traversal — is absent for the same structural reason Cap 3 is unconsumed: a traversal is a single node, so there is no branch fan-out to order, throttle, or partially fail.

Small table (code facts; paths are relative to the repository root `C:/Projects/llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation`, branch `DpoImplementation`, HEAD `0f221ac89`):

| Cap (plain meaning) | Declared? | Consumed / throttles? | Declaration file:line | Consumption file:line |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `MaxCombinations` — how many plans built per input | Yes | Yes — enforced ceiling on `CombinationCount` (not the direct loop counter) | DynamicGraphContracts.cs:559 | AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:134-135; BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:508-509,427; SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:391-392 |
| `MaxParallelCombinationRuns` — concurrent plan-runs | Yes | Yes — real degree-of-parallelism throttle | DynamicGraphContracts.cs:562 | BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135,144-148,162-169 |
| `MaxParallelBranches` — branch concurrency inside one traversal | Yes | No — validated (>=1) and forwarded only; never throttles | DynamicGraphContracts.cs:561 | validate/forward only: SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:393; BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:510,220; AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:136 |

| Required behavior | Status | Evidence file:line |
|---|---|---|
| Deterministic ordering (stable, by plan Index) | Implemented | AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:82-83; BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:160,363-371; test BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunnerTests.cs:80-82 |
| Cancellation (CancellationToken honored) | Implemented | SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:129,152,525-526; BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:136-141,172-181; test ...RunnerTests.cs:446 |
| Timeout | Implemented | SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:561-563,525-526; BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:735-737,649-650; test ...RunnerTests.cs:488 |
| Partial-failure (one plan failing does not abort all) | Implemented (multi-combination runner) | BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:235-240,374-392 |
| Backpressure (degree-of-parallelism limit; no streaming buffer) | Implemented (plan-admission throttle) | BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135,144-148,162-169; stream null at :413 |
| Bounded concurrency | Implemented | BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135,144-148,162-169; test ...RunnerTests.cs:95 |
| Branch concurrency inside one traversal | Absent | no consumer; single-node traversal at SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:83,152,340 |

**Verdict:** R-06 async combinations = PARTIAL. Two of the three caps are real and consumed — `MaxCombinations` as an enforced ceiling on how many plans are built (declared `DynamicGraphContracts.cs:559`, consumed `AxisSweepCombinationStrategy.cs:134-135` / `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:508-509`), and `MaxParallelCombinationRuns` as a genuine concurrency throttle (declared `DynamicGraphContracts.cs:562`, consumed `BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:135,144-148,162-169`, test-proven at `Tests/BoundedCombinationGraphCandidateRunnerTests.cs:95`). The third cap, `MaxParallelBranches` (declared `DynamicGraphContracts.cs:561`), is declared-only in effect: validated (>=1) and forwarded but never throttles anything, because the runners execute exactly one node per plan (`SingleCombinationGraphCandidateRunner.cs:83,152,340`) and so have no in-traversal branches to bound. Of the six required behaviors, five (deterministic ordering, cancellation, timeout, partial-failure, backpressure, bounded concurrency) are implemented at the plan-run level and test-verified in the bounded runner; the branch-level dimension is absent for the same structural reason. The runners also do not delegate traversal to the branch-parallel orchestrator the plan document points to (`AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator` fan-out at its `Task.WhenAll`), which is where a `MaxParallelBranches` consumer would otherwise live.

### Finding 5 — The typed DPO legal chain and the look-alike (Cycle 1, Task 5)

**What was checked.** Whether the plan's fixed, named "legal DPO chain" for producing a training pair exists in the audited code. A "DPO pair" is one training example that pairs a preferred ("chosen") answer against a rejected answer, so the model can be taught to prefer the first. The plan requires that such a trainable pair be reachable ONLY through a fixed sequence of distinctly-named types, each a separate step: a raw comparison row (`GraphDpoSourceRow`) becomes a candidate pair explicitly stamped NOT-ready-to-train (`GraphDpoPairCandidate`, its `DpoTrainReady` flag set to false); only a three-way legal projection carrying full proof and a final go-decision (`TriadLegalProjection`, whose gate token is `G_DPO_FINAL`) may promote that candidate into a real, 14-field, unit-level training pair (`GraphDpoPair`), which then feeds a training item, a dataset load, and one training step. The builder is FORBIDDEN from creating a ready pair directly. Audited code: repository C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, commit 0f221ac89, working tree clean; no build was run; read/grep only.

**What the plan demands (document claims, not code facts).** Plan block 07 states the full typed chain "`GraphDpoSourceRow -> GraphDpoPairCandidate -> TriadLegalProjection(full predicate proof, G_DPO_FINAL) -> GraphDpoPair(Level=unit) -> LearningTrainingItem/JSONL -> DpoDataset.Load -> TrainBatchAsync`" (active-codex-execution-plan-07-dpo-builder.md:29). The same block restates in words that the builder's `BuildCandidateAsync` returns only a not-ready candidate — "возвращает только `OperationResult<GraphDpoPairCandidate(DpoTrainReady=false)>`; builder никогда не создаёт `GraphDpoPair`" (returns only a not-ready candidate; the builder never creates a ready pair) — and that only `TriadLegalProjection`, after full proof and `G_DPO_FINAL`, may create the canonical `GraphDpoPair` (active-codex-execution-plan-07-dpo-builder.md:60-65). Plan block 09 restates the same chain as canon: "`GraphDpoPairCandidate(DpoTrainReady=false) -> TriadLegalProjection(FullDpoTrainReadyProof,G_DPO_FINAL) -> GraphDpoPair(Level=unit,DpoTrainReady=true) -> LearningTrainingItem/JSONL -> DpoDataset.Load -> TrainBatchAsync`; a Candidate never trains directly and the builder never leaps directly to a Pair" (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:9). Both plan files also state their own status in their own words: "dpo_status: NOT_STARTED" and "dpo_priority: LAST" (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:5-6). Those are claims; every statement below is read directly from the audited commit.

**Code fact — four of the five chain types do not exist at all.** A repository-wide search of every C# (`*.cs`) file returns ZERO hits for four of the five named chain types: `GraphDpoSourceRow` (the raw comparison row) — 0 hits; `GraphDpoPairCandidate` (the explicitly not-ready candidate) — 0 hits; `TriadLegalProjection` (the three-way legal projection that alone may promote a candidate) — 0 hits; and `G_DPO_FINAL` (the final go-decision gate token) — 0 hits. None of these names appears in any file, test, or fixture in the repository. The plan's forbidden-shortcut guard (a builder that can emit only a not-ready candidate) therefore has nothing to guard: neither the builder type, nor the candidate type, nor the legal projection, nor the gate token is present in code.

**Code fact — the fifth name, `GraphDpoPair`, exists only as a substring of an unrelated compound type; the canonical pair is absent.** A search for the string `GraphDpoPair` returns 9 occurrences across exactly 4 files, and every single occurrence is the longer compound name `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair`, never a standalone `GraphDpoPair`. A word-boundary search for a standalone `GraphDpoPair` (regex `\bGraphDpoPair\b`) returns 0 hits. The four files all sit inside one unrelated domain folder, `42.ProjectWikiDomain`: `ProjectWikiDomainModels.cs` (2 hits), `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphTrainingRunner.cs` (4), `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphTrainingPipeline.cs` (2), and `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator.cs` (1). So the plan's canonical 14-field, unit-level, legally-projected `GraphDpoPair` does not exist in the code under its own name.

**Code fact — the look-alike `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair` is a different thing, and is a false positive.** This record is defined at `42.ProjectWikiDomain/ProjectWikiDomainModels.cs:923-931` and has 8 fields: `PairId`, `BoundaryMap` (a `SystemBoundaryMapResult`), `A` (the prompt text), `Chosen`, `Rejected`, `RejectedType`, `RequiredPathIds`, and `EvidenceIds`. Its purpose is a preference-training example for a completely different task — teaching a model to build a "cross-stack business-flow graph", a map of how one business flow crosses an Angular client, a .NET server, and SQL stored procedures — as its own generator's prompt states: "Build the business flow graph for one system across Angular client, .NET server, and SQL stored procedures" (`42.ProjectWikiDomain/CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator.cs:58`). That generator's `BuildBatchAsync` (`CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator.cs:19`) constructs these pairs directly from seed data (`:31`), with NO not-ready candidate step, NO legal projection, NO `G_DPO_FINAL` gate, and NO `DpoTrainReady` flag anywhere. It is a name collision only: the grep matched it because `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair` contains the letters `GraphDpoPair` as a substring. It is NOT the plan's graph DPO legal chain, and it is NOT the canonical pair — it is a false positive.

**Code fact — the generic DPO trainer in `11.DPO` contains no legal/provenance concept whatsoever, only numeric preference math.** The generic training-data pair `DpoPair` (`11.DPO/Models/DpoPair.cs:8-13`) is a 3-field record — `Prompt`, `Chosen`, `Rejected` — with no legal, license, provenance, quarantine, teacher, or `DpoTrainReady` field. The loader `DpoDataset.Load` (`11.DPO/DpoDataset.cs:23-31`) simply reads a JSON-lines file and deserializes each line straight into a `DpoPair`; it applies no legal gate, no provenance check, and no quarantine — any well-formed line becomes a training pair. The trainer `DpoTrainer` (`11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs`) is pure preference-optimization arithmetic: it computes the DPO loss `-log σ(β·margin)` from policy and reference log-probabilities (`:41-54`), runs a manual backward pass and an AdamW optimizer step (`:57-62`), and saves metrics and a policy checkpoint (`:75-76`); its inputs are `DpoBatch` token-id/mask tensors, not the plan's typed chain. A targeted, case-insensitive search of `11.DPO` for `legal`, `license`, `provenance`, `quarantine`, `teacher`, and `DpoTrainReady` returns ONLY the AGPL file-license header comment lines at the top of each source file (for example `11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs:2-4` and `11.DPO/DpoDataset.cs:2-4`). That is source-code copyright licensing of the files themselves — it is not a training-data legal, license, provenance, or quarantine concept, and no such domain concept exists anywhere in `11.DPO`.

**Plain answer to the exact question.** The plan's typed legal DPO chain is ABSENT. Four of its five named types (`GraphDpoSourceRow`, `GraphDpoPairCandidate`, `TriadLegalProjection`, `G_DPO_FINAL`) have zero occurrences in the code, and the fifth (`GraphDpoPair`) exists only as a substring of the unrelated `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair`. The earlier-noted look-alike is a FALSE POSITIVE — `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair` is an 8-field pair for cross-stack business-flow-graph training in `42.ProjectWikiDomain`, built directly with no candidate step, no legal projection, and no go-gate. And the generic DPO trainer in `11.DPO` has NO legal/license/provenance/quarantine concept at all — only the numeric preference-training math over `DpoPair`/`DpoBatch`, plus AGPL source-file headers that are unrelated to training-data legality.

**Verdict:** R-07 typed DPO legal chain = MISSING. Four of the five named chain types are absent under any spelling (`GraphDpoSourceRow`, `GraphDpoPairCandidate`, `TriadLegalProjection`, `G_DPO_FINAL` = 0 hits each across all `*.cs`); the fifth exists only as the unrelated compound `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair` (9 hits, 4 files, all in `42.ProjectWikiDomain`; standalone `GraphDpoPair` = 0 hits), which is a false positive (an 8-field cross-stack business-flow-graph pair defined at `ProjectWikiDomainModels.cs:923-931`, built directly by `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator.cs:31` with no candidate / legal-projection / `G_DPO_FINAL` / `DpoTrainReady` step). The generic `11.DPO` DPO trainer carries no legal concept — only numeric preference math (`DpoTrainer.cs:41-76`) over a 3-field `DpoPair` (`DpoPair.cs:8-13`) loaded ungated by `DpoDataset.Load` (`DpoDataset.cs:23-31`); the only `legal`/`license` matches in `11.DPO` are AGPL source-file headers (`DpoTrainer.cs:2-4`), not a training-data legal/provenance mechanism.


### Finding 6 — The legal teacher gate: general vs FLOW-38 (Cycle 1, Task 6)

**What was checked.** Two separate claims in requirement R-08. First, whether the plan's general "legal teacher gate" exists in the audited code: a 20-field `TeacherProvenance` sheet that must accompany every teacher-produced training text (recording who hosted the model, which model and license, whether provider terms were checked, and so on), plus a SEPARATE `JudgeProvenance` sheet for models that only score and never author. Second, whether the one real legal surface that DOES exist — the "FLOW-38 quarantine" handler, which accepts unverified "better-output" evidence from external clients and must keep it quarantined and never auto-trained — is genuinely fail-closed end to end: every write path forcing the train-ready flag to false, and every read path rejecting any stored record whose flag is true. Audited code: repository C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, commit 0f221ac89, working tree clean; no build was run; read/grep only.

**What the plan demands (document claims, not code facts).** Plan block 08 states that this block "owns legal verdicts, the 20-field `TeacherProvenance` compatibility contract, separate `JudgeProvenance`, and legal/provider admission" (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:8). The same file enumerates the exact 20 fields of `TeacherProvenance` — 13 base fields (`teacher_access_mode`, `hosting_provider`, `model_id`, `model_license`, `model_version`, `request_id`, `provider_terms_checked`, `teacher_output_used_for_training`, `closed_proprietary_model`, `source_ref`, `source_sha256`, `contamination_markers`, `judge_model`), 5 benchmark-delta fields (`terms_snapshot_ref`, `geography_export_constraint`, `source_category`, `raw_private_text_copied`, `redaction_policy_ref`), and 2 service-mode fields (`service_mode`, `grounding_used`) — and states that full judge evidence "is carried by separate `JudgeProvenance`" (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:389-409). The document also draws the closed-model boundary: "closed proprietary APIs may judge/evaluate only ... they never author chosen/rejected text, a DPO label, or a training target" (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:11), and the original task lists the teachers banned from authoring — Claude API, OpenAI GPT API, Gemini API, Gemini Grounding/Search (active-codex-execution-original-task.md:83-91). Crucially, the SAME document reports its own implementation status in its own words: "dpo_status: NOT_STARTED" (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:5), and it marks the `JudgeProvenance` carrier and the provider-terms leg as "SPEC CONTRACT DEFINED ... but implementation/runtime evidence is `NEEDS_LEGAL_MACHINE_REVIEW`" (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:13-15). So the plan itself claims a defined spec, not shipped code. Every statement below is read directly from the audited commit.

**Code fact — the general 20-field `TeacherProvenance` and the separate `JudgeProvenance` contracts exist NOWHERE in code.** A repository-wide search of every C# (`*.cs`) file returns ZERO hits for `TeacherProvenance` (0 across 0 files) and ZERO hits for `JudgeProvenance` (0 across 0 files). Two supporting concepts of that contract are likewise absent everywhere: `ProviderTermsProof` (the hosted-terms proof the plan references) — 0 hits; `AdmittedModel` (a legal/provider admission type) — 0 hits. There is no `TeacherProvenance` record, no `JudgeProvenance` record, no 20-field provenance sheet, and no closed-proprietary-model authoring ban implemented in code anywhere in the repository. The general legal teacher gate is therefore absent — exactly as the plan's own "NOT_STARTED" status implies.

**Code fact — the one legal term that DOES appear, `LegalVerdict`, is a single hardcoded string field, not the 20-field contract.** `LegalVerdict` returns 14 hits across 4 files, but it is never a structured provenance contract: it is one `string` field that only ever carries the constant `"QUARANTINE"`. It is defined as a field of the FLOW-38 client-supplied row (`DesignConversionModelServerFlow38QuarantineHandler.cs:36`), of the persisted envelope (`:1212`), and of the public boundary snapshot (`DesignConversionModelServerAspNetHostStartup.cs:35`); every production use pins it to the constant `QuarantineVerdict = "QUARANTINE"` (`DesignConversionModelServerFlow38QuarantineHandler.cs:1232`) and rejects any other value. This is the FLOW-38 quarantine's single verdict flag — NOT the general teacher gate, and NOT a provenance sheet.

**Code fact — the real legal surface is the FLOW-38 quarantine handler, and every WRITE path forces train-ready false.** The handler is `25.ModelServer\DesignConversion\DesignConversionModelServerFlow38QuarantineHandler.cs` (1610 lines). The train-ready flag `DpoTrainReady` occurs six times in that file, classified per occurrence: (1) `:40` — DEFINITION only: the `bool DpoTrainReady` field on the client-supplied `DesignConversionFlow38QuarantinedSourceRow` record. (2) `:221` — FORCE-FALSE: when the store's `AdmitAsync` builds the record it actually persists (`PersistedEnvelope`), it hardcodes `DpoTrainReady: false` regardless of what the client sent. (3) `:517` — PASS-THROUGH: `Snapshot()` copies the client row's `DpoTrainReady` value verbatim, but this snapshot cannot carry a true value into storage because it is fenced by reject-true guards on every side (see next paragraph). (4) `:1216` — DEFINITION only: the `bool DpoTrainReady` field on the internal `PersistedEnvelope` record. So the only field actually written into a persisted record is pinned false at `:221`; the persisted envelope's own `DpoTrainReady` is never derived from client input.

**Code fact — every READ / validate path rejects any record whose train-ready flag is true.** Two reject-true guards fence the write, and one fences the read: (5) `:1422` — REJECT-TRUE: `ValidateRow` returns the error `FLOW38_QUARANTINE_INVARIANT_VIOLATION` ("client evidence must remain ... not train-ready") whenever `row.DpoTrainReady` is true. This guard fires TWICE on the write path — first at the HTTP entry, because `HandleAsync` calls `Validate` before any persistence and returns HTTP 422 on violation (`:1313`), and again inside the store, because `AdmitAsync` calls `IsPersistableQuarantineRow(snapshot)` (which runs `ValidateRow`) and throws `InvalidDataException` before writing a byte (`:177`). (6) `:353` — REJECT-TRUE on read: when the store scans records back from disk, `ParseAndValidateEnvelope` throws "invalid record" if `persisted.DpoTrainReady` is true, and it independently re-runs `IsPersistableQuarantineRow` on the embedded source row (`:342`), so a hand-edited on-disk record with the flag flipped true is rejected on read rather than returned. The public boundary is fail-closed too: the snapshot reads the metadata value through `ReadBoolean(..., expected: false ...)`, which returns false (forced) and flags an issue on any non-false / missing / malformed value (`DesignConversionModelServerAspNetHostStartup.cs:115` and `:248-252`), and the missing-route fallback hardcodes `DpoTrainReady: false` (`:189`). No production path can set the flag true.

**Code fact — the only place the flag is set true is a test asserting rejection.** Across the whole repository, `DpoTrainReady = true` appears exactly once, inside the test `Handler_TrainReadyOrCommonMutation_IsRejectedWithoutPersistence` (`Tests\DesignConversionModelServerFlow38QuarantineTests.cs:97`). That test sends a row with `DpoTrainReady = true` and asserts the handler returns HTTP 422 with error `FLOW38_QUARANTINE_INVARIANT_VIOLATION` and that NO store file is created (`:105-109`) — i.e. it proves the rejection, not a train-ready capability. Every other test occurrence asserts the flag is false (for example `Tests\DesignConversionModelServerAspNetHostTests.cs:244`, `:269`, `:329`, `:338`).

**Plain answer to the exact question.** (a) The general 20-field `TeacherProvenance` sheet and the separate `JudgeProvenance` sheet do NOT exist anywhere in the code (both 0 hits across all `*.cs`, as are `ProviderTermsProof` and `AdmittedModel`); the only legal term present, `LegalVerdict`, is a single hardcoded `"QUARANTINE"` string on the FLOW-38 path, not a provenance contract — so the general legal teacher gate is absent, matching the plan's own "NOT_STARTED" status. (b) The FLOW-38 quarantine handler IS genuinely fail-closed end to end: the only persisted flag is forced false on write (`:221`), the train-ready invariant is rejected before persistence at both the HTTP entry (`:1313` -> `:1422`) and the store (`:177` -> `:1422`), stored records with the flag true are rejected on read (`:353`, plus `:342`), the public boundary snapshot forces false (`:115` / `:248-252`, `:189`), and the single true-setting in the whole repo is a test that asserts rejection (`:97`, `:105-109`).

**Verdict:** R-08 legal teacher gate = PARTIAL — split exactly as predicted. The GENERAL 20-field legal gate is MISSING: `TeacherProvenance` = 0 hits, `JudgeProvenance` = 0 hits, `ProviderTermsProof` = 0, `AdmittedModel` = 0 across all `*.cs`, and the plan itself reports `dpo_status: NOT_STARTED` (active-codex-execution-plan-08-legal-teacher-gate.md:5) with the `JudgeProvenance` / terms legs still `NEEDS_LEGAL_MACHINE_REVIEW` (:13-15). The FLOW-38 quarantine gate is REAL and genuinely FAIL-CLOSED end to end: `DpoTrainReady` is DEFINITION at :40 and :1216, FORCE-FALSE at :221, PASS-THROUGH-but-fenced at :517, and REJECT-TRUE at :353 (read-back) and :1422 (row invariant, fired on the write path at both :1313 and :177); `LegalVerdict` is pinned to the constant `"QUARANTINE"` (:1232); and the only `DpoTrainReady = true` in the repo is the rejection test at Tests\DesignConversionModelServerFlow38QuarantineTests.cs:97 (asserts 422 + FLOW38_QUARANTINE_INVARIANT_VIOLATION + no persistence at :105-109). No production path can set train-ready true.

### Finding 7 — The auto-DPO loop and what 16.AutonomousLearning really is (Cycle 1, Task 7)

**What the plan promises (document claim).** The plan block titled "Блок плана 09 — Авто-DPO петля" literally calls this loop "сердце проекта", the heart of the project (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:52). In plain words the plan describes one closed self-improvement loop: run one of our real projects, watch where our small local model scores low, take a stronger allowed outside ("teacher") model's answer as the goal to reach, build a DPO preference pair (a "correct" chosen answer versus a plausible "wrong" rejected answer) out of that gap, train on it, measure on held-out tasks, and repeat until quality is good enough. The original task states the same idea in one sentence: we run any of our projects and see where the model gave a low result, we take what the external model produced as the goal, and we train until we start getting a sufficiently good result (active-codex-execution-original-task.md:15). The plan names the classes that would make up this loop and tags them NEW, meaning they are to be built, not existing: the loop controller `AutoDpoLoopOrchestrator`, the low-score detector `IGraphWeaknessDetectorUnit`, the teacher-goal capturer `ITeacherGoalProviderUnit`, and the pair builder `IGraphDpoPairBuilderUnit` (plan-09 lines 170-177 and 235-237).

**(a) Does the loop exist in code? No — the loop and its two signature stages are absent (code fact).** I searched every C# file in the checkout (C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, HEAD 0f221ac89, clean working tree). The strings "AutoDpo" and "auto_dpo" produce zero hits anywhere in the repository, so the named loop controller `AutoDpoLoopOrchestrator` does not exist. The low-score / weakness detector that would feed a DPO builder does not exist: `IGraphWeaknessDetectorUnit`, `GraphWeakness`, and any "Weakness" detector class all return zero hits (the single repo-wide "weakness" match is a code comment describing the `[WRONG: weakness - gap - consequence]` text format of a rejected answer at 27.TranslationDomain\TranslationUnitContracts.cs:140 — that is a label format, not a detector). The teacher-goal capture stage does not exist either: `ITeacherGoalProviderUnit`, the canonical `ITeacherGoalClient`, the method `GetGoalAsync`, and the string "TeacherGoal" all return zero hits. There is no repeat-until-quality controller: no class loops DPO rounds until a quality threshold, and the plan's runtime-observation record `LowResultObservation` returns zero hits. So the connective control loop the plan calls the heart of the project is not implemented in any form.

**What does exist are separate, disconnected building blocks the loop would have consumed (code fact).** These pieces are real, but none of them is the loop, and nothing wires them into a loop:
- The DPO math engine `DpoTrainer` is real and performs the gradient step (11.DPO\DpoTrainer.cs:16); it is invoked by the capability-training path (18.CapabilityTraining\LlmIngredientLogicalUnits.cs:360), not by any loop.
- The top-of-graph learning machine `DynamicGraphMachine`, which the plan says the loop should own, exists (15.OrchestratorModel\Logical\DynamicGraphMachine.cs:22) but is explicitly an "L5 ownership scaffold. Training lifecycle methods deliberately fail until a real trained path exists" (:21). Its `TrainBatchAsync`, `EvaluateAsync`, `ExportAsync`, and `ImportAsync` each return a failure with a "...NOT_ACTIVATED" code (:45-77); its constants read `RuntimeMode = "honest_untrained"` and `SemanticQualityClaimed = false` (:25-26). So even the machine that would host the loop cannot train yet.
- The "run one input through several different combinations" step (the loop's step 1) exists only as an interface contract, not a running implementation: `IGraphCandidateRunner.RunCandidatesAsync`, `GraphCandidateRunRequest`, `AsyncCandidateBatch`, and `CombinationPlan` are declared in 00.Core\DynamicGraphContracts.cs:545-614, and the file states plainly that "concrete execution and scheduling remain outside this slice" (:608).
- A DPO-pair builder that produces `GraphDpoPair`-style rows does exist, but in a different project and driven by static seeds rather than by low-score observation: `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator` (42.ProjectWikiDomain\CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoGenerator.cs:10) builds a fixed batch of 50 pairs from a hardcoded `BuildSeeds` list using template `BuildChosen`/`BuildRejected` text. It is a domain fixture generator, not a loop fed by a running model's weak outputs.

**(b) What is 16.AutonomousLearning really? A different, earlier autonomous-learning feature (code fact).** It is an overnight, idle-triggered SUPERVISED LoRA fine-tuning cycle over harvested .NET knowledge — it is not the DPO loop. LoRA means Low-Rank Adaptation, a lightweight way to fine-tune a model by training small adapter weights instead of the whole model. Each of the eight subfolders does one plain job:
- Core (Interfaces.cs) defines the shared records and interfaces of the cycle: the machine-state snapshot, the trust level, the data-source entry, the system monitor, the cycle-state storage, the data-source finder, the training-data provider, the accuracy evaluator, and the CycleResult output.
- DataSourceFinder lists the .NET learning sources to read from (the GitHub repos dotnet/runtime, aspnetcore, efcore, roslyn, plus Microsoft docs), grouped into trust tiers (DataSourceFinder.cs:20-32).
- IdleDetector is the "is the laptop safe to train on right now" check: it reports safe only when CPU is under 20%, there has been no keyboard or mouse input for 10 minutes, the battery is above 50% or charging, and at least 2 GB of disk is free (IdleDetector.cs:46-66).
- Pipeline (AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs) is the 8-step cycle orchestrator: idle check, measure baseline accuracy, collect labeled examples, apply class-imbalance weighting, train a LoRA adapter, measure the new accuracy, and register the adapter only if accuracy went up (AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:72-159).
- Preprocessing holds DatasetPreprocessor, which turns raw source text into a labeled training example after a stack-detection gate (DatasetPreprocessor.cs:45-92), and DatasetWeighter, which applies the class-imbalance weight formula (DatasetWeighter.cs:26-36).
- Training holds AutonomousTrainer, which wraps the incremental LoRA trainer with a replay mix (AutonomousTrainer.cs:49-79), and ReplayBuffer, which keeps roughly 70% new and 30% old examples so the model does not forget what it already learned (ReplayBuffer.cs:39-59).
- TrustScorer scores each source's trustworthiness with a weighted feature formula and buckets it into High, Medium, or Low (TrustScorer.cs:78-101).

**What 16.AutonomousLearning trains or produces (code fact).** It produces domain LoRA adapters. Each training row is `TrainingExample(Prompt, Response, Domain, Weight, ...)` where the prompt and the response are the SAME normalized source text (DatasetPreprocessor.cs:79-84) — that is ordinary supervised text learning, not a chosen-versus-rejected preference pair. The cycle returns a `CycleResult` carrying baseline accuracy versus enhanced accuracy plus the saved adapter path (Interfaces.cs:80-85), and it promotes the adapter only when enhanced accuracy exceeds baseline (AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:142-145).

**Does anything inside 16.AutonomousLearning reference the dynamic graph or DPO pairs? Almost nothing (code fact).** A grep of the whole folder for "DynamicGraph" returns zero hits — nothing in 16.AutonomousLearning knows about the dynamic graph, `DynamicGraph*` types, or `ILearningMachine<DynamicGraphDomain>`. It never mentions `GraphDpoPair`. The only DPO reference is a dormant pass-through: `AutonomousTrainer.TrainAsync` accepts a generic `IReadOnlyList<DpoPair> dpoPairs` parameter (AutonomousTrainer.cs:52) and forwards it to the incremental trainer (:66), but the pipeline that actually drives the cycle always calls it with an EMPTY list `[]` (AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:125). So the DPO channel here is a wire that is never fed; no DPO pair is ever built or trained inside this folder.

**Different feature, not the loop — with only a pattern-level overlap (verdict basis).** 16.AutonomousLearning is a DIFFERENT autonomous-learning feature; it is not the plan's auto-DPO loop and not a partial build of it. The only overlap is that the plan borrows one small idea from it as a precedent (in the plan's Russian, образец, meaning a model to imitate): the "promote only if the new score beat the old score" delta-gate. The plan says so explicitly, citing this folder as the model for the loop's promotion step (plan-09:176 and plan-09:469, which points at AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:137-140; in this HEAD that same gate sits at :142-145). Beyond that shared promotion pattern and the unused DpoPair parameter, the two share nothing: 16.AutonomousLearning does supervised LoRA on .NET docs, whereas the auto-DPO loop is meant to do preference training on a dynamic graph from a model's own low-score runs measured against a teacher goal.

**Verdict:** R-09 auto-DPO loop = MISSING — the closed loop the plan calls the heart of the project is not implemented; its three signature parts (low-score detection feeding a DPO builder, teacher-goal capture, and a repeat-until-quality controller) all return zero code hits, and the L5 machine that would host it is an honest-untrained scaffold whose training is deliberately not activated (DynamicGraphMachine.cs:21-52). Only disconnected prerequisite blocks exist (the `DpoTrainer` engine at 11.DPO\DpoTrainer.cs:16, an `IGraphCandidateRunner` interface with no execution at 00.Core\DynamicGraphContracts.cs:609, and a static seed-based `GraphDpo` generator in 42.ProjectWikiDomain). Separately, 16.AutonomousLearning is a DIFFERENT, earlier feature — an overnight idle-time supervised LoRA fine-tuning cycle for .NET domain knowledge — that references neither the dynamic graph nor `GraphDpoPair`, carries only an empty/unused `DpoPair` parameter (AutonomousTrainer.cs:52 fed `[]` at AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:125), and overlaps the plan only as the cited precedent for the promotion delta-gate.


### Finding 8 — Is the two-phase teacher route truly zero? (Cycle 1, Task 8)

**What this task checks, in plain words.** The plan's "teacher" is a stronger outside AI model whose answers are treated as the training target the local model should learn to match; the requirement labelled R-16 says this teacher must be rolled out in two separate phases. Phase 1, which the plan names `TP1_LOCAL` (a short code meaning "teacher phase 1, run locally"), is a Qwen3.5-9B model — an open-weight model from the Qwen model family, in a compressed form called Q4_K_M — running on the developer's own machine through Ollama, which is a tool for running language models locally, under a settings profile the plan names `qwen35-local-v1`. Phase 2, which the plan names `TP2_HOSTED` ("teacher phase 2, hosted"), is a Qwen3 model hosted by an outside neutral provider called TogetherAI, under a profile named `qwen3-together-v1`, and it may begin only after a technical checkpoint the plan names `G-TP-4` (a named gate) is passed. This task asks whether any of that exists as real client code, configuration, or endpoints anywhere in the repository across every file type — not only C# source files — or whether it is genuinely not started.

**What the documents claim.** The plan documents describe this two-phase teacher route in detail but state plainly that it has not been built yet. The single-owner overlay document records the stage state as "TP1_LOCAL_NOT_STARTED; TP2_HOSTED_NOT_STARTED" and the training state as "NOT_STARTED" (active-codex-execution-two-phase-local-remote-overlay.md:11-12). The two-phase addendum defines Phase 1 as the local Qwen3.5-9B Q4_K_M teacher in Ollama under profile `qwen35-local-v1`, and Phase 2 as the hosted `qwen3-together-v1` at TogetherAI (active-codex-execution-original-task.md:394-397). The model-policy document repeats the same two profile names and confirms the hosted phase stays blocked behind the `G-TP-4` gate (active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:21, 33-41). So the documents describe the route and, in the same breath, declare it not started.

**What the code contains (code fact).** A case-insensitive search of every text file the repository tracks — 86,420 files, tracked plus untracked-but-not-ignored, with build output folders bin/ and obj/ and the git internals .git/ excluded — was run for eleven teacher-route terms. All of the route's own identifiers return zero hits everywhere: the phase codes `TP1_LOCAL` and `TP2_HOSTED` = 0, the gate `G-TP-4` = 0, both profile names `qwen35-local-v1` and `qwen3-together-v1` = 0, and the neutral-provider endpoint forms togetherai, api.together, together.ai, and dashscope (dashscope is a rival hosted-model endpoint) all = 0. The only two terms with any hits at all are the plain words "ollama" (101 lines) and "qwen" (35 lines), and every one of those 116 hits sits in a non-code file: 90 in experiment data files with the .jsonl extension (a data format that stores one record per line), 10 in generated .json result manifests, 15 in Markdown documentation and plan files, and 1 in a captured editor cache file. Zero of the 116 hits appear in a C# source file (.cs), a project or build file (.csproj, .props, .targets), or any configuration, YAML, XML, environment, shell, or PowerShell script.

**What those "ollama"/"qwen" hits actually are (code fact).** They are prose and stored data, not a client. In the experiment .jsonl files the word appears inside captured conversation text such as "even without Ollama installed" (experiments-v2/XIIGEN-DOCS-BATCH-COMPARISON/run-2026-05-08-093325/rows-200-fixed.jsonl:1412). In the generated manifests it appears inside a descriptive sentence, "Claude Code handles infrastructure (Ollama, ES, Docker)" (experiments-v2/TEACHING-XIIGEN-SESSIONS-BATCHED/results/run-2026-05-06-144220/checkpoints/.../batch-0030-plan/plan/4-3/generated_capability_manifest.json:12). The single .cache hit is a captured Cursor/VS Code editor profile that lists third-party AI-extension vendors, one of which happens to be named "ollama" (artifacts/extension-runtime-token-quality-learnability/phase-10-cursor-full-extension-live-proof/.../extensions.builtin.cache) — that is a captured copy of an unrelated editor's own settings, not this project's code. The "qwen" hits in docs are plan references naming Qwen3 (Apache-2.0 licence) as a candidate teacher, for example docs/plans/claude-ILEARNINGDYNAMICGRAPH-DPO-PLAN-v1.md:788 and the licence note in CLAUDE.md:1393. None of these hits is a teacher-route client, a configuration value, or an endpoint.

**No disguised teacher call over plain HTTP (code fact).** The C# code does make outbound web requests: there are 372 lines mentioning HttpClient (the .NET class used to call a web address) across 27 source files. None of them point at an external model host — every known model endpoint string returns zero in .cs files (api.openai.com, api.anthropic.com, generativelanguage.googleapis, api.together, dashscope, huggingface.co, replicate.com are all = 0, and Ollama's default local port 11434 = 0). Every BaseAddress (the target address a web client is aimed at) resolves to the project's own in-process or self-hosted test application address — for example ResolveStartedAddress(app) and a baseUrl value read back from the app's own ready-root — never to an outside model provider. So there is no generic web client that could be a teacher call in disguise.

**Verdict:** R-16 two-phase teacher route = MISSING (not started) — the teacher route is genuinely zero across all file types: no Ollama, Qwen, or TogetherAI client, config, or endpoint exists in any C# or configuration file, and all five route identifiers (`TP1_LOCAL`, `TP2_HOSTED`, `G-TP-4`, `qwen35-local-v1`, `qwen3-together-v1`) plus every neutral-provider host string (togetherai, api.together, together.ai, dashscope) are absent everywhere in code, with the only "ollama"/"qwen" hits confined to experiment data, documentation, and one captured editor cache; this matches the plan's own recorded state (TP1_LOCAL_NOT_STARTED; TP2_HOSTED_NOT_STARTED), so it is an honest not-started gap and not a claimed-only overclaim.

### Finding 9 — How far do the measurement contracts go? (Cycle 1, Task 9)

Plain words: requirement R-11 asks that learning be proven with a number — a held-out score (accuracy measured on data the trained thing never saw), an ablation (the same score with the learned part switched off, compared against a plain deterministic baseline), and a contamination status (proof that the training data and the evaluation data do not overlap) — and that those numbers be attached ("bound") to the saved trained result, not merely written into a log. This task checked whether the measurement record types declared in one core file are actually produced and consumed by real code, or whether they are empty shapes that nothing fills.

What the file declares (code fact). The file 00.Core/DynamicGraphMeasurementContracts.cs declares 28 types: 2 enums and 26 records. The two enums are DynamicGraphMeasurementTargetLevel (the graph layer being measured: Pair, L2 unit, L3 flow, L4 orchestrator, L5 learning-machine) at line 11 and DynamicGraphMeasurementMechanismKind (what mechanism sits behind a target; only the value "LearningSubject" may be admitted, and the other values — Knn, Scorer, Counter, Regex — are named as forbidden shortcuts) at line 25. The records include the target identity DynamicGraphMeasurementTarget (line 38), the contamination evidence DynamicGraphMeasurementSplitProof (line 56, which is the train/eval source-root split plus a cross-split near-duplicate count), the novelty input DynamicGraphNoveltyObservation (line 69) and its measured form DynamicGraphNoveltyMeasurement (line 84), the accuracy pieces DynamicGraphAccuracyAxis and DynamicGraphAccuracyMeasurement (lines 112 and 122, which deliberately separate a "live margin" accuracy from a "held-out" accuracy), the ablation DynamicGraphAblationMeasurement (line 135), the two speed families (lines 164-221), the two edge-quality families (lines 246 and 289), the untrained-honesty family (lines 347-401), and a set of "Target..." wrapper records that each pair a measured value with the target identity.

Every declared type is filled by real code — none is a dead shape (code fact). Searching the whole repository for construction sites (a `new X(...)`), 26 of the 26 records are constructed in production (non-test) code, and both enums are used in production. The producing code is a set of "meter" classes under 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical: the accuracy meter builds DynamicGraphAccuracyMeasurement at DynamicGraphAccuracyMeter.cs:75, the ablation meter builds DynamicGraphAblationMeasurement at DynamicGraphAblationMeter.cs:66 and :113, the novelty meter builds DynamicGraphNoveltyMeasurement at DynamicGraphNoveltyMeter.cs:51, the split guard re-owns a validated DynamicGraphMeasurementSplitProof at DynamicGraphHeldOutSplitGuard.cs:76, the run-speed and training-speed meters build their measurements at DynamicGraphRunSpeedMeter.cs:76 and DynamicGraphSpeedMeter.cs:37, the two edge-quality meters produce theirs at DynamicGraphEdgeOrderQualityMeter.cs:103 and DynamicGraphEdgeIdentityQualityMeter.cs:94, and the untrained-honesty probe and meter produce theirs at DynamicGraphUntrainedHonestyL2Probe.cs:149/162/163 and DynamicGraphUntrainedHonestyMeter.cs:118. A real novelty observation is produced with computed rates at DynamicGraphEdgeAuthorityNoveltyEvaluation.cs:497 (the new-pattern rate and the seen-token rate are computed just above, at :492-493). All the "Target..." wrappers are produced by one composing class, DynamicGraphMeasurementCoordinator.cs (for example the target-novelty wrapper at :293, target-ablation at :236, target-accuracy at :264, and the L5 target-training-speed at :342). So the measurement records are live: each has a real producer and a real consumer, and none is a declaration-only shape.

One input type is only produced by tests (code fact). The single exception is DynamicGraphRunSpeedObservation (declared at line 188): the only `new DynamicGraphRunSpeedObservation(...)` in the entire repository is in Tests/DynamicGraphRunSpeedMeterTests.cs:236-237; no production code constructs a run-speed observation, although production code (the run-speed meter and the coordinator's MeasureRunSpeed) does consume one when it is handed in. This is a narrow gap: the run-speed measurement path has a real consumer but, at present, only a test-authored input.

What the numbers actually are (code fact, important nuance). These meters largely validate and re-shape numbers handed in by the caller, rather than compute them from a live trained model running over held-out data.
