The runner constructs candidates, writes NDJSON, runs the schema validator, and emits an evidence folder; its own lifecycle-evidence output states `common_checkpoint_mutated = false`, `promotion_to_user_adaptive_store = "not_performed_staged_only"`, and `fresh_load_or_continue_training_claim = "not_claimed_by_phase_07_slice"` (ResultGapPhase07Runner\Program.cs:309-320). No code path feeds these NDJSON rows into `TrainBatchAsync` or `LearnPair`. So the candidates are authored and staged to disk as train-ready material, but nothing turns them into training.

On the privacy/validation fence versus the plan's legal chain: the code fact is that the router (ResultGapDpoWriters.cs:116) quarantines external/vendor or unapproved sources (:121, reason `external_vendor_or_unapproved_source`), quarantines forbidden or raw-private privacy scopes (:124), routes personal/user-correction sources to `adaptive_only` so they can never enter common training (:127-140), and only routes approved-common / repository-common / synthetic sources with an allowed license (MIT, Apache-2.0, ProjectInternal, or Synthetic — :197-201) and common-allowed privacy to `trainable_common` (:142-155); the validator enforces 14 fields including no raw private text, no raw vendor output, no common-checkpoint-mutation claim, technical refs excluded, source id not used as a training feature, and a train-ready near-miss taxonomy code (ResultGapDpoWriters.cs:246-262). The document claim it is the closest analogue of is the plan's not-ready-candidate concept `GraphDpoPairCandidate`: per the plan this is a NEW class that does NOT exist in the graph code, whose builder returns only `GraphDpoPairCandidate(DpoTrainReady=false)` and never a `GraphDpoPair`, and only `TriadLegalProjection` after full proof and the `G_DPO_FINAL` gate may mint the canonical `GraphDpoPair`, after which a separate materializer builds the JSONL/batch for `TrainBatchAsync` (active-codex-execution-plan-07-dpo-builder.md:60-65); Finding 5 records that `GraphDpoPairCandidate` is absent from the repo. The mapping: the ResultGap writer is a domain-LOCAL analogue of that discipline (author a candidate, mark it train-ready or quarantine, never auto-promote, fence by license/privacy), and its fence rhymes with the plan's row-validity/legal/split gates, but it is a smart-skills convention, not the graph's typed legal chain — it emits `ResultGapTrainableDpoCandidate` rather than `GraphDpoPairCandidate`, has no `TriadLegalProjection`, no `G_DPO_FINAL`, and no teacher-provenance gate, and (as above) nothing downstream converts its train-ready rows into a `GraphDpoPair` or a training batch. It is the nearest in-repo shape to the plan's concept while remaining a parallel, self-contained convention that stops at "staged on disk."

On the `metadata_hash_repair` history: the data fact is that `models\index.json` carries `metadata_hash_repair` blocks for prompt-to-task-v1 (index.json:52-67) and skill-selection-v1 (index.json:100-119), each preserving the `previous_checkpoint_sha256` and `previous_manifest_sha256`, the current values, a `repair_id`, a timestamp, and explicit `semantic_quality_claimed:false` and `learned_behavior_changed` flags — false for the prompt-to-task repairs, but true for the skill-selection `prompt_context_negative_rejection_repair` (index.json:117). The prompt-to-task checkpoint file itself embeds the same audit trail with a plain-language reason: a field (`learnedState.manifestSha256`) had carried the source corpus-summary hash while being read as model-manifest metadata, so it was removed and an explicit non-embedded current-hash policy was recorded (prompt-to-task-v1-checkpoint.json:530-551). The code fact is that the machines verify these hashes at load — `PromptToTaskMachine.ValidateManifestAsync` compares the manifest's `CheckpointSha256` to the actual file hash (PromptToTaskMachine.cs:717-719) and `ImportAsync` rejects a hash mismatch (PromptToTaskMachine.cs:374-375) — so a drifted hash had to be repaired for the artifact to load at all. What the history implies: a non-destructive, auditable integrity discipline — every prior sha256 is retained with a reason, a timestamp, and an explicit statement of whether learned behavior, metrics, or semantic-quality changed. That is strong artifact-integrity hygiene, though the repeated repair cycles also show the hash bookkeeping was initially inconsistent and needed several corrective passes.

**Verdict:** smart-skills machines = curated-pattern / counting materializers, NOT gradient-trained models — PromptToTask and OrchestrationReasoning materialize authored pattern lists (entry method `MaterializeAsync`, hard-coded confidences, `TrainingStarted=false`, OrchestrationReasoningTrainer.cs:34, :187, :97) and SkillSelection accumulates signed-integer skill-profile tallies plus KNN memory plus a `Dictionary<int,int>` candidate-position counter (LearnPair, SkillSelectionMachine.cs:737, :772), with zero gradient/optimizer code anywhere in `43.SmartSkillsDomain`; the `held_out_accuracy = 1` on only 4 rows is presented honestly (row counts disclosed, `semantic_quality_claimed=false` both stamped in `index.json` and enforced at load via PromptToTaskMachine.cs:634/:714 and OrchestrationReasoningMachine.cs:639/:724) but is a small-sample pass, not proof of a semantic model; the SkillSelection lifecycle IS a real fresh-load round-trip — train, export to files, fresh instance import, reproduce the corrected `plan-review` decision from typed learned state, sourced from the semantic units and not the scaffold weights (proven by Tests\SkillSelectionMachineTests.cs:38-140, esp. :118-131); and the ResultGap DPO candidates are authored, license/privacy-fenced and 14-field schema-validated and staged to disk as train-ready material but NOTHING consumes them into training (only the writer, `tools\ResultGapPhase07Runner\Program.cs`, and tests touch them; runner declares staged-only, Program.cs:309-320) — a domain-local analogue of the plan's absent `GraphDpoPairCandidate` not-ready concept (active-codex-execution-plan-07-dpo-builder.md:60-65), not the graph's typed legal chain; the `metadata_hash_repair` records show a non-destructive, auditable hash-integrity discipline (one repair flagged `learned_behavior_changed=true`, index.json:117).


### Finding 24 — Design-conversion: the closest sibling to the plan's legal DPO (Cycle 3, Task 5)

This finding audits the design-conversion subsystem (folder `44.DesignConversionDomain`, which turns a Figma/Canva design into HTML/CSS and grades it, plus its model-server surface `25.ModelServer\DesignConversion\`). It is the one place in the repository that combines real DPO gradient-training machinery with the legality wrapper (approval gates, promotion gates, a held-out measurement gate, and the FLOW-38 quarantine already audited in Findings 6 and 15). The question is how close it comes to the plan's target: a legally-gated, fresh-loaded, held-out-measured DPO. "DPO" here means Direct Preference Optimization, the training method that nudges a policy model toward a "chosen" answer and away from a "rejected" one. Plain answer up front: it really runs one real gradient DPO step on a toy model and really fresh-loads the result, but the preference pairs are self-authored synthetic material, held-out measurement never runs in production, and every path is stamped "not product ready" — so it is the nearest living relative to the plan's DPO, but a parallel stepping-stone, not the plan's legally-gated held-out-measured production DPO.

**(1) TRAINS — yes, one real gradient step on a tiny toy model, fed by self-authored synthetic pairs.**

The code fact is that the bridge does construct and run a real gradient trainer. `DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.ExecuteAndConvertAsync` builds a `DpoTrainer` at DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs:88 (via `CreateDpoTrainer`, which calls `new DpoTrainer(policy, reference, dir, 1e-5f)` at :198-202 — a real policy model, a real reference model, and a learning rate), then runs a single gradient step `trainer.StepVector(batches, 1)` at :92, then writes the policy checkpoint file whose name is `DpoTrainer.BuildPolicyCheckpointFileName(1)` at :96 and fails with `DESIGN_CONVERSION_DPO_POLICY_CHECKPOINT_NOT_WRITTEN` if that file is absent (:97-100). The ingredient id `"11.DPO:DpoTrainer.StepVector"` — the label recording that the real DPO trainer's gradient step was used — is stamped into the result's `IngredientIds` at :158 and into the evidence refs at :344. So this is genuine gradient training, not a metadata copy.

The code fact is that the trained model is a deliberately tiny toy. `CreateTinyPolicyConfig` (DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs:183-193) uses `VocabSize = 512`, `HiddenDim = 16`, `NumLayers = 1`; the bridge takes at most `MaxBridgePreferenceCount = 8` preference rows (:36, :71) and runs exactly `DpoStepCount: 1` (:146). The bridge stamps its own honesty markers into the evidence: `DESIGN_CONVERSION_DPO_POLICY_BRIDGE_PRODUCT_READY_FALSE` (:341) and `DESIGN_CONVERSION_DPO_POLICY_BRIDGE_NO_RAW_PAYLOAD` (:342). So the training is real but scaffold-scale and self-declared not-product-ready.

The code fact is that the pairs feeding this trainer are self-authored synthetic material, not two real distinct product outputs judged against each other. `DesignConversionDpoGenerator.BuildRuntimeCreatedStoredExample` (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1114) sets `chosen` to the machine's OWN runtime output (hashed refs of `evaluation.ImprovedHtml`/`ImprovedCss`, DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1125-1143, `Accepted: true`) and sets `rejected` to a synthetic baseline derived from the submitted request itself (hashed `request.Html`/`request.Css` + "baseline", DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1144-1167, `Accepted: false`), with the rejected score simply defined as chosen minus 0.01 (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1123). The record is tagged `NegativeReasonTaxonomy = SyntheticRuntimeNotTrainReady` (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1182) and carries `synthetic_negative=true`, `scaffold_negative=true`, `training_grade=false`, `production_dpo=false`, `product_not_ready=true`, `held_out_not_proven=true`, `source_disjoint_not_proven=true` (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1197-1216, mirrored in the warnings at DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:975-984). The generator's other entry, `BuildTrainReadyRecords` (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1079), can accept externally-supplied `DesignConversionStoredDpoExample` rows that could in principle carry real distinct chosen/rejected outputs, but the runtime self-service path builds the pair from one output plus a degraded copy of the input. The dataset builder that would normally precede training refuses to mark anything product-ready: every preflight constructor (`LocalFixtureTrainingCandidateOnly`, `FromRealS3Validation`, etc.) sets `ProductReady: false` and `TrainingCandidateOnly: true` (DesignConversionDpoDatasetBuilder.cs:82-207), and the default count gate demands 50 train + 50 held-out records (DesignConversionDpoDatasetBuilder.cs:210-216). So design-conversion trains, but on its own synthetic seeds, at candidate-only status.

**(2) RELOADS-FRESH + MEASURES — named fresh-load and continue-training probes exist and run, but only on throwaway scaffold checkpoints, and no held-out measurement ever runs in production.**

The code fact is that this domain owns the repository's only NAMED fresh-load and continue-training probes, and they do real work. `DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe` (DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs:27) saves then reloads a checkpoint through `VerifySavedCheckpointAsync` (:31) and `VerifyCheckpointFileAsync` (:48), reloading via `DesignConversionMachine.LoadCheckpointAsync` and checking machine-id/domain/version/manifest continuity and whether real learned weights survived. `DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.VerifyFreshLoadedCheckpointAsync` (DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:48) loads the fresh checkpoint, rejects any batch that reuses a held-out record identity (DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:115-118), trains a train-split-only continuation batch, saves it to a SEPARATE path, and proves the source checkpoint is byte-unchanged and the batch was appended exactly once. But the code fact is that both probes hard-code the measurement and promotion legs to off: the fresh-load report always sets `MeasurementGateRun: false`, `ContinueTrainingProven: false`, `PromotionUploadAllowed: false` (DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs:96-98) with evidence `DESIGN_CONVERSION_LOCAL_FRESH_LOAD_DOES_NOT_RUN_MEASUREMENT_GATE` (:104), and the continue-training report always sets `HeldOutRecordUsageCount: 0` (DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:223), `MeasurementGateRun: false`, and `PromotionUploadAllowed: false` (DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:232-233).

The code fact is that these probes ARE wired into a production class, but they run only against an ephemeral scaffold checkpoint. `DesignConversionTrainingPipeline` calls `VerifySavedCheckpointAsync` at DesignConversionTrainingPipeline.cs:446 and `VerifyFreshLoadedCheckpointAsync` at :463, but the checkpoint path is a temp directory built by `BuildLocalFreshLoadProbeTempDirectory(options.RunId)` at :442 and unconditionally deleted in a `finally` by `TryDeleteLocalFreshLoadProbeTempDirectory` at :494. Excluding `Tests\`, the only other references to the probes are status-string comparisons: `DesignConversionLocalArtifactDb.cs:7723` reads `DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.VerifiedStatus` and inspects the continue-training report at :7802, and `DesignConversionModelServerTrainingIntakeHandler.cs:1089` only references the `VerifiedStatus` constant. So the probes run on a temporary checkpoint that is created and destroyed inside the pipeline call — never against a persistent, served production model.

The code fact is that the runtime learner never measures held-out quality at all: the comment at DesignConversionMachine.cs:889-890 states "TrainBatchAsync does not perform held-out measurement; it carries forward existing accuracy while checkpoint evidence records NOT_MEASURED markers." The measurement gate itself, `DesignConversionMeasurementGateEvaluator.Evaluate` (DesignConversionMeasurementGate.cs:60), is a strict evaluator that requires a held-out record count, a held-out quality score at or above threshold, runtime/cost/RAM within budget, a source-disjoint proof, no leakage, no hidden determinism, and a passed ablation (DesignConversionMeasurementGate.cs:84-161) — but it evaluates metrics that are handed to it in `request.HeldOutMetrics`; it does not itself run a measurement. The pipeline feeder `EvaluateMeasurementGateAfterLocalContinueTraining` (DesignConversionTrainingPipeline.cs:648) returns null when no request is supplied (:655-656) and returns a blocked failure when continue-training is not verified (:664-684). Nothing in the self-authored runtime path computes a real held-out quality score from the gradient policy, so the gate stays not-run or fails.

The document claim this bears on is the plan's open-gate list. The plan states, in words, that "reference-checkpoint, reward-margin, held-out/source-disjoint evaluation, lifecycle/fresh-load/continue-training … gates remain OPEN" (active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:12). Design-conversion has built the NAMED scaffolding for exactly those gates — fresh-load probe, continue-training probe, source-disjoint split, a held-out measurement gate — but because they run on temporary scaffold checkpoints and on externally-supplied (never runtime-produced) metrics, they are consistent with the plan's "OPEN": the doors exist and are strict, but nothing real walks through them in production.

**(3) LEGALITY — the strongest wrapper in the repo: judge-only external AI, non-DPO human approval, and promotion gates that block by default.**

Beyond the FLOW-38 quarantine, this domain enforces four additional legality layers. The code fact is that `DesignConversionModelAuthorityBoundary` (DesignConversionModelAuthorityBoundary.cs:10-13) hard-codes `trained_model_authority = "False"` and `not_trained_model_authority = "True"` — the trained model is defined to have no authority. The code fact is that promotion is heavily gated: `DesignConversionPromotionGateEvaluator.Decide` (DesignConversionPromotion.cs:99) rejects on a hidden shortcut (:107-108), a deterministic shortcut (:110-111), a failed held-out source-disjoint check (:113-114), a failed budget tier (:116-117) or a missing required artifact (:119-120), quarantines when the DPO quarantine count exceeds its max (:128), rolls back when fresh-load failed (:134-138), and only returns `Promote` when every gate passes (:157-160); and the publisher adds four more upload blocks — it rejects the upload unless the measurement gate passed (DesignConversionPromotion.cs:245), the source-disjoint held-out readiness gate passed (:262), the checkpoint contains real trained weights (:277), and lifecycle evidence allows the upload (:291) — with the default uploader being local-only. The code fact is that human approval is explicitly separated from DPO: `DesignConversionHumanApprovalService.ApprovalBoundary` is the literal string `design_conversion_non_dpo_human_approval_refs_only` (DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:461), and every approval record is built with `ProductReady: false`, `TrainedOrLearnedProvenance: false`, `DpoEligible: false`, `ModelWeightsPublished: false` (DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:599-602, echoed in the evidence at :631-633). So a human approving a rendered design never marks that design as trained, DPO-eligible, or published as weights.

The code fact is that the external visual AI is kept strictly judge-only. `DesignConversionExternalVisualJudgeNetwork` (DesignConversionExternalVisualJudgeNetwork.cs:12) is purely a hardened HTTP client for reaching an external visual judge — it pins DNS resolution, blocks disallowed destination addresses (DesignConversionExternalVisualJudgeNetwork.cs:117-122), disables proxies and auto-redirects (:38-44) — and it authors nothing; it only carries a request to a judge. In the approval path the external visual AI comparison is admitted as references only: the review kind is `external_visual_ai_comparison_receipt_refs_only` (DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:464), `diff_png_fabricated` is set false and the artifact must carry a server-owned receipt proving a real external provider call (DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:864-937) before it is trusted. The document claim this satisfies is the plan's authoring boundary, in words: closed proprietary APIs "may judge/evaluate only where allowed; they never author `chosen`, `rejected`, a DPO label, or a training target" (active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:11). The code matches that claim — the external AI produces verdicts/scores/receipts, and the chosen/rejected text is always the project's own output plus a self-authored baseline, never the external model's writing.

**(4) HANDOFF — the ModelServer's real Logical-DPO handoff is test-only; the wired path is a local, gated, self-declared-not-ready scaffold.**

The code fact is that `25.ModelServer\DesignConversion\DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.ExecuteAsync` (DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs:55) is a heavier, more realistic DPO path: it runs training through `new LogicalDpoTrainingUnit().ExecuteAsync` (DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs:114) with a real policy model, reference model, tokenizer, and optional LoRA config; it consumes the step-vector ingredient at DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs:140; and it scans for policy checkpoint files at :262-266 (`FindPolicyCheckpointFilePaths`, matching `DpoTrainer.PolicyCheckpointFilePrefix`). Its quality gate, however, is an in-sample ablation: `EvaluateLocalAblationQualityGate` (DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs:384) computes the average chosen score minus the average rejected score over the training rows themselves (:404-406) against a 0.10 threshold (:53) — not a held-out measurement — and stamps `DESIGN_CONVERSION_DPO_HANDOFF_NO_REAL_TRAINED_WEIGHTS_CLAIM` (:427) and `DESIGN_CONVERSION_DPO_HANDOFF_PRODUCT_READY_FALSE` (:428). The code fact is that this ModelServer handoff is not wired into any endpoint: excluding its own file, the only reference to `DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff` in the repository is `Tests\DesignConversionModelServerApiTests.cs`. So the most realistic DPO training path in the domain is built-but-unwired — exercised only by a test.

The code fact is where the actually-wired training leads. The domain bridge `DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.ExecuteAndConvertAsync` is called from `DesignConversionLocalArtifactDb.cs:3013`, inside the local-promotion path — but only after `trainingRun.ModelUploadReady` is true (DesignConversionLocalArtifactDb.cs:2988; otherwise the run is blocked at :2990-2999 with `local_promotion_blocked_model_upload_not_ready`), and its output is then run through the full `DesignConversionPromotionPublisher` gates with a default `LocalOnlyTrainingIntakePromotionUploader` (DesignConversionLocalArtifactDb.cs:3027) — i.e. local only, no remote model-server upload. That local artifact DB is in turn hosted by a LOCAL server: it is wired into `DesignConversionModelServerApi.cs`, `DesignConversionModelServerEndpointRegistry.cs`, `DesignConversionModelServerAspNetHostStartup.cs`, and the developer tool `tools\DesignConversionLiveLocalServer\Program.cs`.

On whether a working end-to-end train -> save -> fresh-load -> measure chain exists in production: the train -> save -> fresh-load legs exist and run (the bridge's `StepVector` -> `BuildPolicyCheckpointFileName(1)` file -> `freshPolicy.LoadCheckpoint` at DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs:92-103, and the pipeline's runtime-machine save/fresh-load/continue-train probes), but the MEASURE leg does not run in production: the runtime machine carries accuracy forward as NOT_MEASURED (DesignConversionMachine.cs:889), the bridge performs no held-out evaluation of the trained policy, and the measurement gate is fed only externally-supplied metrics. Because the pairs are self-authored synthetic, the trained model is a toy, held-out measurement never executes, and every artifact is stamped product-not-ready / no-real-trained-weights, this is a self-contained parallel scaffold that rehearses the plan's chain rather than a living production instance of it.

**Verdict:** design-conversion DPO = the plan's NEAREST LIVING RELATIVE but a parallel stepping-stone, not the legally-gated fresh-loaded held-out-measured production DPO the plan targets. It genuinely TRAINS — one real gradient `DpoTrainer.StepVector` step on a 1-layer/16-hidden toy model, writing a real policy checkpoint (DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs:88, :92, :96, :158, :344) — but from SELF-AUTHORED SYNTHETIC pairs (chosen = the machine's own output, rejected = a degraded copy of the request; `SyntheticRuntimeNotTrainReady`, `product_not_ready=true`, DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1114-1216), not real distinct product outputs. It RELOADS-FRESH through the repo's only named probes (DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs:31/:48, DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:48), and they DO run in the production training pipeline — but only against a temp scaffold checkpoint that is deleted immediately (DesignConversionTrainingPipeline.cs:442/:494), and both probes hard-code `MeasurementGateRun=false` (DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs:97, DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs:232). It does NOT MEASURE held-out in production: `TrainBatchAsync` records NOT_MEASURED (DesignConversionMachine.cs:889) and the strict measurement gate (DesignConversionMeasurementGate.cs:60) only evaluates externally-supplied metrics that the runtime path never produces. Its LEGALITY is the strongest in the repo — external visual AI is strictly JUDGE-ONLY and authors nothing (DesignConversionExternalVisualJudgeNetwork.cs, receipt-refs-only at DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:464/:864-937), human approval is explicitly non-DPO (`design_conversion_non_dpo_human_approval_refs_only`, `DpoEligible=false`, DesignConversionHumanApproval.cs:461/:599-602), the trained model is granted no authority (DesignConversionModelAuthorityBoundary.cs:10-13), and promotion blocks unless real trained weights + measurement-gate + source-disjoint held-out readiness all pass (DesignConversionPromotion.cs:245/:262/:277) — matching the plan's authoring boundary at active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:11. The train->save->fresh-load chain exists and runs on a LOCAL model server, but the held-out MEASURE leg is absent in production and the most realistic Logical-DPO handoff (DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs:55/:114/:140/:266) is TEST-ONLY (sole non-self caller: Tests\DesignConversionModelServerApiTests.cs). Map: stepping stone + parallel DPO scaffold with the repo's best legality fence — NOT a working end-to-end legally-gated held-out-measured production DPO, consistent with the plan's own "gates remain OPEN" (active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:12).


### Finding 25 — The trainable router: does anything behind the word Train actually learn? (Cycle 3, Task 6)

**Scope in plain words.** This finding audits the project folder named "26.TrainableRouter" — the folder whose name promises a router that *learns* which processing unit a piece of work should be sent to. The single question here is whether the word "Train" inside this folder means real machine learning (a model whose numeric weights are adjusted by gradient descent) or deterministic bookkeeping (counting words, with no weights and no learning). "Routing" here means: pick which unit (a small specialist model, e.g. an orchestration-planner, a translation unit, a documentation unit) should own an incoming request.

**(1) The "trainer" is deterministic keyword counting, not learning.**
The code does this: the method `RoutingProfileTrainer.Train` (26.TrainableRouter\Training\RoutingProfileTrainer.cs:27) takes a list of worked examples and, for each one, calls a private helper `ExtractKeywords` (same file, line 70) that lower-cases the text, splits it on spaces and punctuation, drops words shorter than three letters and a fixed stop-word list, and collects the surviving words into a per-unit `HashSet<string>` (a plain set of distinct words). What it returns is a dictionary of "unit id -> the set of words seen in that unit's examples" (lines 54-67). There is no weight, no gradient, no loss function, no optimizer, and no checkpoint — nothing numeric is adjusted by any example. The word "Train" is a label on a routine that assembles a bag of keywords per unit.
The code confirms this again at routing time: `KeywordUnitScorer` (26.TrainableRouter\Training\KeywordUnitScorer.cs:11) scores a candidate unit by counting how many of that unit's stored keywords appear in the request text — `hits = profile.Keywords.Count(keyword => text.Contains(keyword))` (line 48) — then adds fixed constant bonuses for a matching domain (+20.0) or matching direction (+2.0) (lines 49-54). This is arithmetic over a word list, not a learned function.
A document claims the matching thing: an earlier Cycle 1 review (Finding 2 of this same report) already classified `KeywordUnitScorer` as one of the deterministic mechanisms the plan wants replaced. The plan document that demands that replacement is active-codex-execution-plan-12-no-hidden-determinism.md; its heading at line 36 reads (translated from Russian) "Plan block 12 — Replacing hidden determinism (classification of 9 mechanisms + cutover to a *trainable* edge authority)." So the plan's own words put this class of keyword mechanism in the "hidden determinism to be cut over" bucket, not in the "learned thing" bucket.

**(2) The DPO preference pairs are produced, but nothing trains on them.**
Background in plain words: "DPO" (Direct Preference Optimisation) is a real training technique that consumes "preference pairs" — for each decision, one *chosen* answer and one *rejected* answer — and then nudges a model's weights so the chosen answer becomes more likely than the rejected one. This folder contains two builders that manufacture such pairs.
The code does this: `RoutingDpoPairBuilder.BuildPairs` (26.TrainableRouter\Training\RoutingDpoPairBuilder.cs:12-39) turns each logged routing decision into a `RoutingDpoPair` record holding a chosen sentence ("Route to X ...") and a rejected sentence ("Route to Y ... [WRONG: ...]"). `OrchestrationPromptDpoPairBuilder.BuildPromptFilePairs` (26.TrainableRouter\Training\OrchestrationPromptDpoPairBuilder.cs:12) does the same for orchestration-prompt scenarios. Each builder only *returns* an array of pairs; neither one calls any trainer.
Where the conveyor ends (the code shows the whole path): I grepped the entire repository for every consumer of the `RoutingDpoPair` and `OrchestrationPromptDpoPair` types. The complete consumer list is: the two runners `RoutingTrainingRunner.cs:84` and `CodeDocumentationTrainingRunner.cs:104`, which build the pairs and then merely place the array into a result record (as the `DpoPairs` field of `RoutingTrainingRunResult` / `CodeDocumentationTrainingRunResult`); plus exactly one unit test each — `Tests\DistributedModelArchitectureTests.cs:270` and `Tests\TrainableRouterOrchestrationTests.cs:490` — that assert only that the pairs were built with the right chosen unit and a "[WRONG:...]" annotation (see the assertions at DistributedModelArchitectureTests.cs:297-301). The third runner named in the task, `OrchestrationEvaluationRunner.RunPromptFileGateAsync` (26.TrainableRouter\Training\OrchestrationEvaluationRunner.cs:25), does not build pairs at all — it only scores routing accuracy and phase coverage and returns a metrics report. So the pairs are a dead leaf of the conveyor: they are produced and stored in a result object, and no code path ever feeds them into a weight-mutating trainer.
The code leaves a tell-tale dead shape: this same folder defines a record `RoutingTrainingResult` (26.TrainableRouter\Models\RoutingModels.cs:89) with the fields `PairsBuilt`, `PairsTrainedOn`, `RewardMargin`, `DpoAccuracy`, and `Promoted` — precisely the shape a genuine DPO run would fill in. A repository-wide grep shows this record is never constructed anywhere; the field `PairsTrainedOn` is written by no line of code. The type anticipates training that never happens in this folder.
The contrast proves the gap: a real gradient DPO trainer does exist elsewhere in the same repository — `11.DPO\DpoTrainer.cs` computes a reward margin from chosen-minus-rejected log-probabilities (lines 70, 127, 173, 227), and `Tests\DpoTrainerTests.cs:251` proves that margin actually improves after training. The router's `RoutingDpoPair` type never appears anywhere inside `11.DPO` (the grep returns zero references there), and the whole `26.TrainableRouter` folder contains zero occurrences of gradient / optimizer / DpoTrainer / StepVector / loss / weight-update terms. So the router's pairs never reach `11.DPO`'s gradient trainer, nor any other trainer that mutates weights.

**(3) Mapping to the plan, and legality.**
A document claims the target: the plan's edge-authority block, active-codex-execution-plan-02-edge.md line 24, is headed (translated from Russian) "Plan block 02 — Graph edge: `ILearningFlow` ≡ `ILogicalFlow` (L3) + *LEARNED* edge authority." In plain words, the plan wants the routing choice — which unit owns the graph edge — to be decided by a *learned* unit.
Measured against that target, 26.TrainableRouter is BOTH of the two offered options, i.e. answer (c): (a) it is the deterministic baseline the plan says must be demoted, because its live routing decision is made by keyword counting in `KeywordUnitScorer`; AND (b) it is a pair-production stepping stone, because its two builders emit chosen/rejected DPO pairs in the exact shape a future graph-DPO trainer could consume — even though today nothing consumes them for weight training.
Legality (the code carries none): I grepped 26.TrainableRouter for any legality/provenance concept — the words provenance, teacher, legal, quarantine. The expected count was zero and it is effectively zero. The single textual hit is a *training-example sentence* inside the routing curriculum ("Translate a Hebrew legal phrase into English." / "legal translation", 26.TrainableRouter\Training\OrchestrationRoutingCurriculum.cs:603) — that is dataset content used as an example prompt, not a mechanism. None of the router's outputs (the keyword profiles, the unit scores, the routing decisions, or the DPO pairs) carry any provenance, teacher-legality, or quarantine field. The router has no legality/provenance concept at all.

**Verdict:** trainable router = deterministic keyword bookkeeping wearing the word "Train" — `RoutingProfileTrainer.Train` (RoutingProfileTrainer.cs:27, :70) builds bag-of-keyword profiles and `KeywordUnitScorer` (KeywordUnitScorer.cs:11, :48) routes by counting keyword hits, with no weights and no gradient; its DPO pairs are produced but never trained on — `RoutingDpoPair` / `OrchestrationPromptDpoPair` reach only result records and two builder unit tests, never `11.DPO`'s gradient `DpoTrainer` (zero references in 11.DPO) or any weight-mutating trainer, and the `RoutingTrainingResult.PairsTrainedOn` shape (RoutingModels.cs:89) is defined but never populated; mapped to the plan it is BOTH (c) the deterministic edge-authority baseline that active-codex-execution-plan-02-edge.md:24 says must become a LEARNED unit AND a pair-production stepping stone a future graph DPO could consume; and it carries zero legality/provenance concept (the only "legal" token is inside a translation training-example sentence at OrchestrationRoutingCurriculum.cs:603).


### Finding 26 — The supervised LoRA chain: can the overnight learner produce an adapter? (Cycle 3, Task 7)

**What this finding checks.** Finding 7 established that the folder 16.AutonomousLearning is a separate feature from the DPO work: it is an idle-time "supervised" fine-tuning cycle (it is meant to run overnight when the machine is idle) whose DPO input wire is always fed an empty list. "LoRA" (Low-Rank Adaptation) is a technique that trains a small set of extra "adapter" weights bolted onto a frozen base model instead of retraining the whole model; an "adapter" is the small file of those extra weights. Finding 7 never checked whether this LoRA training chain actually works. This finding follows the chain from 16.AutonomousLearning down into the LoRA math and answers four questions: does the chain apply an adapter and run a real gradient step, does it save and reload an adapter, is the promotion gate comparing real numbers, and does the code match its own comments.

**(1) Does it apply adapter weights and run a real gradient step? No — the training method is a stub.** The overnight trainer delegates the real work to one method, `IncrementalTrainer.TrainAsync` (a "gradient step" is the calculation that nudges weights to reduce error — the single operation that actually constitutes learning). The code does this: at 14.ContinuousLearning\Training\IncrementalTrainer.cs:48-57, after two guard checks (empty domain, empty example list), the method's entire body is to build a file-path string `Path.Combine(_adapterDir, "{domain}.lora")` (line 55), run `await Task.CompletedTask` (line 56 — a no-op that awaits nothing and does no work), and return that path string as success (line 57). It never touches a model. A repository grep for the terms Backward, Optimizer, ".Step(", AdamW, or SGD across all three folders (10.LoRA, 14.ContinuousLearning, 16.AutonomousLearning) returns only comment text, not one executable call — so no gradient step runs anywhere in the chain. The LoRA math primitives themselves are real but sit unused by this chain: the code shows `LoraApplicator.Apply` (10.LoRA\LoraApplicator.cs:17-45) really does freeze the base weights and build `LoraLinear` adapter objects, and `LoraLinear.Forward` (10.LoRA\LoraLinear.cs:52-64) really computes the low-rank adapter output — but `LoraLinear` defines only a Forward method and no Backward, and a grep shows `LoraApplicator.Apply` is called only from the 18.CapabilityTraining folder and the Tests folder, never from 14 or 16. The chain cannot even reach that code: `LoraApplicator.Apply` requires a live `LlmModel` (an in-memory language model) as its first argument, and a grep of folders 14 and 16 for `LlmModel` finds only two comment lines (IncrementalTrainer.cs:20 and :54) and zero actual model construction.

**(2) Does any path save a LoRA adapter to disk and reload it? No.** The code does this: the only disk write anywhere in the three folders is `DomainDatasetCache.cs:68`, which serializes the training *dataset* (the collected examples), not an adapter; the string `.lora` appears only as a path assembled at IncrementalTrainer.cs:55 and as a comment describing a record field at 14.ContinuousLearning\Core\Core.cs:45. There is no `File.Write` of adapter weights, no adapter serializer, and no adapter loader anywhere in the chain. A filesystem search of the whole repository for `*.lora` or `*.adapter` artifacts returns nothing — no adapter file has ever been produced and committed. The pipeline's own test double confirms the intent: the stub file-writer used in the pipeline tests, `NullCodeWriterAL` (Tests\AutonomousLearningTests.cs:608-612), "reports success without writing anything." So nothing is saved and nothing is reloaded.

**(3) Is the promotion gate real, or is it comparing numbers no adapter produced? It compares numbers no real adapter produced.** The promote-on-improvement gate is at 16.AutonomousLearning\Pipeline\AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:142-145: if `enhanced <= baseline` it refuses to promote and returns the error code ACCURACY_NOT_IMPROVED. Both numbers come from the same call, `_evaluator.Evaluate(domain)` — baseline at line 91 (before training) and enhanced at line 139 (after). The code shows two independent reasons the "enhanced" number cannot reflect a real trained adapter. First, the candidate adapter object built at lines 131-135 (a `ModelAdapter` record) is never passed to the evaluator — `Evaluate` takes only a domain string (interface at 16.AutonomousLearning\Core\Interfaces.cs:75) — and no adapter file was ever written for it to load. Second, and decisively, there is no production implementation of the accuracy evaluator at all: the only class in the entire repository that implements `IAccuracyEvaluator` is a test stub, `SequentialAccuracyAL` (Tests\AutonomousLearningTests.cs:590-604), which simply dequeues a pre-configured list of numbers — the first call returns the baseline value the test chose, the second call returns the enhanced value the test chose. The interface's own comment states this plainly; a document claim, in the source comment at 16.AutonomousLearning\Core\Interfaces.cs:70, reads: "Test stubs return configurable values to verify before < after assertion." So in this repository the gate is real *control flow* over two doubles, but the two doubles can only be hand-fed test values — never a score any trained LoRA adapter produced, because no adapter is ever trained, saved, or scored here.

**(4) Comment-vs-code drift — the wiring the comments promise does not exist.** A source comment claims the wiring is done elsewhere: at 14.ContinuousLearning\Training\IncrementalTrainer.cs:53-54 the comment reads, "LoraApplicator.Apply and DpoTrainer.Step are wired up in 16.AutonomousLearning where a live LlmModel is available." The header comment above it (IncrementalTrainer.cs:11-20) similarly lays out a four-step training recipe ("freeze base, return adapters" / a cross-entropy pass / "DpoTrainer.Step(batch, step) per DpoPair" / "save adapter weights to adapterDir/{domain}.lora"), and a sibling comment at 14.ContinuousLearning\Fabric\Interfaces.cs:45 says the trainer "Delegates to LoraApplicator.Apply from 10.LoRA." The code contradicts all of it: a grep of the entire 16.AutonomousLearning folder finds zero references to `LoraApplicator`, zero to `DpoTrainer`, and zero `LlmModel` construction. The 16-folder trainer, `AutonomousTrainer.TrainAsync` (16.AutonomousLearning\Training\AutonomousTrainer.cs:49-79), does replay-buffer mixing and then calls only `_trainer.TrainAsync` at line 66 — that is, the stub described above — and the pipeline never builds a model or applies or steps anything. This is genuine comment-vs-code drift and is reported here as a finding, not a footnote: the comments describe a wired supervised-LoRA-plus-DPO pipeline, while the code is a path-string stub with no model, no adapter, and no gradient. ("DPO," Direct Preference Optimization, is a separate real gradient trainer that lives at 11.DPO\DpoTrainer.cs and is exercised from other folders — but the comment's specific claim that its Step is wired into 16.AutonomousLearning is false.)

**Mapping to the plan.** A document claims this subsystem is only a precedent: consistent with Finding 7, the plan cites 16.AutonomousLearning solely as the *sample* for the promote-on-delta gate — active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md line 176, the step-6 line reading "шаг 6 промоушен по дельте + повтор → гейт дельты (образец 16.AutonomousLearning) → 25.ModelServer", where "образец" is Russian for "sample/pattern." The same plan section (its honest-boundary paragraph beginning at active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:179) explicitly says the loop must NOT reinvent the DPO math because "она уже есть и работает — 11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs" ("it already exists and works — 11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs"). So the only thing reusable from this subsystem is exactly what the plan claims: the *shape* of the promote-only-if-improved delta gate (the `enhanced <= baseline` reject-otherwise control flow at AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:142-145). Nothing downstream of that gate — apply, gradient step, adapter save, adapter reload, real scoring — is reusable, because in this repository none of it exists.

**Verdict:** supervised LoRA chain = a hollow pipe. It does NOT apply an adapter or run any gradient step — `IncrementalTrainer.TrainAsync` (14.ContinuousLearning\Training\IncrementalTrainer.cs:48-57) only returns a "{domain}.lora" path string after a no-op `await Task.CompletedTask`, and grep finds zero Backward / optimizer / ".Step(" / `LlmModel` construction anywhere in 10.LoRA + 14 + 16 (the real primitives `LoraApplicator.Apply` and `LoraLinear.Forward` exist in 10.LoRA but are called only from 18.CapabilityTraining and Tests, never from this chain); it does NOT save or reload an adapter (no `File.Write` of adapter weights, no loader, and no `*.lora`/`*.adapter` file exists anywhere in the repo); the promotion gate at AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:142-145 is real control flow but compares two doubles that can only come from the test stub `SequentialAccuracyAL` (Tests\AutonomousLearningTests.cs:590-604) — the sole `IAccuracyEvaluator` in the repository — never from a real adapter, which is never trained, saved, or scored; and the comment at IncrementalTrainer.cs:53-54 claiming "LoraApplicator.Apply and DpoTrainer.Step are wired up in 16.AutonomousLearning" is aspirational and contradicted by the code (zero LoraApplicator / DpoTrainer / LlmModel references in 16.AutonomousLearning). The plan (active-codex-execution-plan-09-auto-dpo-loop.md:176) reuses only the delta-gate idea, exactly as stated — nothing else in this chain is reusable.


### Finding 27 — The legal-policy documents versus the training that really exists (Cycle 3, Task 8)

**Plain-language framing.** This finding holds the two governing legal-policy documents up against the REAL training that Cycle 3 Tasks 1-7 (Findings 20-26) found actually running in the code, and asks whether the documents tell the truth about it. The first document, `active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md`, is the "who may author training data and when" policy: it records that the plan's own DPO is `dpo_status: NOT_STARTED` (:5) and `dpo_priority: LAST` (:6), lays out a canonical order in which real DPO training happens only at the very end after a final legal gate — `CODE_FIRST -> TP1_LOCAL_EVAL_ONLY -> TP2_HOSTED_EVAL_ONLY (only after G-TP-4) -> G_DPO_FINAL -> DPO_TRAINING` (:8) — and draws the authoring boundary that chosen/rejected training text "may be authored only by the project's own deterministic graph/self-best route or by a permitted open-weight model" while "Closed proprietary APIs ... never author chosen, rejected, a DPO label, or a training target" (:11). "TP1" and "TP2" are the two teacher phases named in that sequence: TP1 is a stronger language model run locally, TP2 a hosted one, each meant to supply the training target the local model should learn to match. The second document, `active-codex-execution-05-dpo-data-training-measurement-guide.md`, is the "how the data is shaped and measured" guide: it fixes the preference-pair shape (:30), the rule that training rows and held-out rows must come from source-disjoint groups (:65), and the anti-deterministic-training warning that a "learned" claim needs, among other proofs, held-out rows, a positive reward margin, and a fresh-load checkpoint pass (:100-109), reinforced by its quality-gates table (`split_gate` :134, `lifecycle_gate` :137). Three checks follow, each written as document-claim versus code-fact.

**(1) VIOLATION CHECK — does any real Cycle-3 training author chosen/rejected text with an external or closed model? No, and this is proven, not assumed.** The document claims the authoring boundary at active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:11: only the project's own deterministic route or a permitted open-weight model may author chosen/rejected material, and a closed proprietary API may "judge/evaluate only" and "never author." The code, across every real trainer Cycle 3 found, authors its pairs itself. The project-wiki runner builds chosen and rejected deterministically from seed templates with no external corpus read anywhere (Finding 20; `BuildChosen`/`BuildRejected` at 42.ProjectWikiDomain\CodeTraceBuilderDpoGenerator.cs:77/:120). The capability trainer's chosen text is a hard-coded template string (Finding 21; `BuildChosenCapabilityText` at 18.CapabilityTraining\DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:685). The translation machine authors its chosen/rejected from its own license-checked corpus (Finding 22). Design-conversion sets chosen to the machine's OWN output and rejected to a degraded copy of the submitted request, never to an external model's writing (Finding 24; 44.DesignConversionDomain\DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs:1114-1167). The router builds its pairs deterministically as "Route to X" / "Route to Y [WRONG:...]" sentences (Finding 25). The code cross-check confirms no external author entered any of this: every DPO model manifest stamps `external_ai_used: false` (models\index.json:20 for orchestration-reasoning-v1, :49 for prompt-to-task-v1; models\skill-selection\skill-selection-v1\manifest.json:66), and the training machinery HARD-REJECTS any manifest that reports external AI — `OrchestrationReasoningTrainer` aborts the whole run with code `ORCHESTRATION_REASONING_TRAINER_EXTERNAL_AI_REJECTED` when a manifest carries `external_ai_used=true` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\OrchestrationReasoningTrainer.cs:63-64). The code shows the only two tracked files where `external_ai_used: true` appears are a runtime-QA report and a self-reflection note, not model manifests, and even there the same record explicitly declares `dpo_generation: false` and `training_or_checkpoint_mutation: false` (artifacts\extension-runtime-token-quality-learnability\codex-runtime-qa-report.json:76 alongside :72) — i.e. the external AI drove a runtime QA probe, never authored a training pair. Finding 8 already established the deeper fact that no external model endpoint exists in the code at all (every model-host string returns zero hits in .cs files, and every HttpClient target is the project's own self-hosted address). So there is NO authoring-boundary breach. The one place a real external AI is reached is the design-conversion domain, and it is reached strictly as a JUDGE that authors nothing (Finding 24; review kind `external_visual_ai_comparison_receipt_refs_only`, `DesignConversionExternalVisualJudgeNetwork` carries a request but writes no chosen/rejected) — which is exactly the judge/evaluate-only use the boundary at :11 permits.

**(2) SCOPE-HONESTY CHECK — read the :9 "nothing calls TrainBatchAsync" claim precisely.** The document claims, at active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:9, "current_effective_train_ready: false; neither TP1 nor TP2 starts DPO, emits a train-ready pair, calls `TrainBatchAsync`, or promotes a checkpoint." Read precisely, the grammatical subject of that sentence is "neither TP1 nor TP2" — the two TEACHER-phase routes named in the canonical sequence at :8 — so the claim is a statement about the teacher-authored graph-DPO path, NOT a claim that no `TrainBatchAsync` anywhere in the repository ever runs. The code shows the teacher route is genuinely absent (Finding 8: no local-Qwen or hosted-Qwen client, config, or endpoint exists across all file types), and the plan's OWN target graph machine deliberately fail-closes — `DynamicGraphMachine.TrainBatchAsync` returns `Fail("Dynamic graph training is not activated.")` (15.OrchestratorModel\Logical\DynamicGraphMachine.cs:45-52). But the code ALSO shows that real, running `TrainBatchAsync` implementations exist in several PARALLEL domain subsystems that are neither TP1 nor TP2: `TranslationMachine.TrainBatchAsync` (27.TranslationDomain\TranslationMachine.cs:50, Finding 22), `OrchestrationPlanMachine.TrainBatchAsync` (42.ProjectWikiDomain\OrchestrationPlanMachine.cs:134, the KNN sibling of Finding 20), `DesignConversionMachine.TrainBatchAsync` (44.DesignConversionDomain\DesignConversionMachine.cs:61, which is actually called by the design-conversion gradient bridge at DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs:106 and by the training pipeline at DesignConversionTrainingPipeline.cs:253, Finding 24), and `SkillSelectionMachine.TrainBatchAsync` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\SkillSelectionMachine.cs:307, Finding 23), plus three smart-skills adapter hierarchies (ResultGap/PromptToTask/OrchestrationReasoning `LogicalHierarchy.cs`). Separately, the project-wiki gradient DPO of Finding 20 runs real training through `DpoTrainer.StepMany` without going through `TrainBatchAsync` at all. So: do these real runs CONTRADICT the document as written? No — they contradict only a CARELESS reading that drops the "TP1 nor TP2" qualifier and hears "nothing anywhere calls TrainBatchAsync." As written, the :9 claim is TRUE — the teacher route calls no `TrainBatchAsync`, and the graph machine's own `TrainBatchAsync` is a fail-closed stub — while the parallel-domain trainers are a separate, honestly-out-of-scope fact the sentence never denies. This distinction matters for the human reader: the correct summary is "the teacher-authored graph DPO is zero and the graph machine itself fail-closes, yet the repository still contains multiple real, running domain trainers," NOT "the document lied about TrainBatchAsync."

**(3) GATE-MATCH CHECK — score the strongest real training against the guide's own three named gates.** The document claims three gate conditions in the guide: a source-disjoint train/held-out split (active-codex-execution-05-dpo-data-training-measurement-guide.md:65 and `split_gate` :134), a positive held-out reward margin (the anti-deterministic warning line :107 and the DPO-metrics pass meaning "Policy prefers chosen over rejected on held-out rows" :93), and a fresh-load checkpoint pass (`lifecycle_gate` :137 and the warning's fresh-load line :109). The two strongest real runs score in complementary pieces, and neither passes all three. The project-wiki DPO (Finding 20) SATISFIES the source-disjoint split — its training-domain and held-out-domain name lists are disjoint (42.ProjectWikiDomain\CodeTraceBuilderDpoGenerator.cs:360-361, with 50 separate held-out rows) — and SATISFIES the positive held-out reward margin, though barely (heldOutRewardMargin ~0.0019 positive, heldOutAccuracy 0.60), but it FAILS the fresh-load gate, because the saved `policy_final.bin` is never reloaded into a fresh model and re-scored (one write site at ProjectWikiDpoMetricRunner.cs:219, zero read sites). The design-conversion DPO (Finding 24) is the mirror image: it OWNS the repository's only named fresh-load and continue-training probes and they DO run inside the production training pipeline (44.DesignConversionDomain\DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs, DesignConversionLocalContinueTrainingProbe.cs) — but only against a temporary scaffold checkpoint that is deleted immediately, and both probes hard-code `MeasurementGateRun: false` (DesignConversionLocalFreshLoadProbe.cs:97), while the runtime learner records held-out quality as NOT_MEASURED (DesignConversionMachine.cs:889) and its self-authored synthetic pairs are stamped `source_disjoint_not_proven=true` (DesignConversionDpoGenerator.cs). So the guide's three named gates are each satisfied by SOME real run — split plus positive margin by project-wiki, fresh-load machinery by design-conversion — but NEVER all three together on one run, which is exactly what the policy's own open-gate list predicts: "reference-checkpoint, reward-margin, held-out/source-disjoint evaluation, lifecycle/fresh-load/continue-training ... gates remain OPEN" (active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:12).

**Verdict:** policy vs reality = CONSISTENT. Every real Cycle-3 training stays inside the authoring boundary (self-authored deterministic pairs; external AI used only as a non-authoring judge; `external_ai_used: false` on every model manifest and hard-rejected if true — active-codex-execution-04-dpo-legal-model-policy.md:11 versus models\index.json:20/:49 and 43.SmartSkillsDomain\OrchestrationReasoningTrainer.cs:63-64), so there is NO authoring violation. The :9 "neither TP1 nor TP2 ... calls TrainBatchAsync" claim is TRUE as written (the teacher route is absent per Finding 8, and the plan's own graph machine's TrainBatchAsync fail-closes at 15.OrchestratorModel\Logical\DynamicGraphMachine.cs:45-52); it is contradicted only by a CARELESS reading that ignores the "TP1 nor TP2" scope, because the real running TrainBatchAsync/DPO trainers live in parallel domains the sentence never covers. And the guide's three gates (source-disjoint split :65/:134, positive held-out margin :107/:93, fresh-load :137/:109) are each met by some real run but never all three at once, matching the policy's own "gates remain OPEN" (:12). The single largest mismatch is one of EMPHASIS, not truth: the two documents present DPO as `NOT_STARTED` / `LAST` / all-gates-OPEN, yet the repository already contains multiple real, running gradient-DPO and TrainBatchAsync trainers (project-wiki's held-out-measured gradient DPO, design-conversion's fresh-load-probed gradient bridge) — the documents UNDERSTATE how much real parallel training exists, but because that training is explicitly outside the plan's typed graph chain and inside the authoring boundary, it is an honest understatement rather than a contradiction.


### Finding 28 — Fresh-gap hunt: uncovered trainers, artifact provenance, and contradictions (Cycle 3, Task 9)

**Plain-language framing.** This is a deliberate hunt for gaps that Findings 1-27 never touched, in five areas: (1) a headcount of every "training" class to see whether any trainer was missed, (2) whether the published models trace back to code that lives in THIS copy of the repository or were carried in from somewhere else, (3) whether the plain-English README or the CLAUDE.md developer guide claim more training ability than the code actually has, (4) training dials declared in the code that nothing ever reads, and (5) whether two different parts of the system quietly disagree about the same file on disk. Plain glossary: a "trainer" is any class whose job is to teach a model; "DPO" (Direct Preference Optimization) is the training method that nudges a model to prefer a good answer over a bad one; a "checkpoint" is the saved-weights file a trainer writes; a "manifest" is the small description file that records what a checkpoint contains. Git state was verified clean before starting (`git status --porcelain` empty) at HEAD 0f221ac89, and the working tree was left untouched (read/grep only).

**(1) TRAINER CENSUS — one genuine, complete training loop was never audited, and it turns out to be dead code.** The code does this: `04.TrainingLoop\Trainer.cs:24` declares `class Trainer`, whose method `TrainAsync(LlmModel model, AdamW optimizer, DataLoader loader)` at Trainer.cs:36 runs the full classic supervised training loop on a real in-memory language model — it takes a batch, computes the forward pass to logits (`model.Forward`, Trainer.cs:56), computes cross-entropy loss (Trainer.cs:57), runs a manual backward pass (`logits.Backward`, Trainer.cs:129), clips the gradients (`TensorOps.ClipGradNorm`, Trainer.cs:65), takes a real AdamW optimizer step (`optimizer.Step`, Trainer.cs:68), zeroes the gradients (Trainer.cs:69), and periodically writes a weight checkpoint (`model.SaveCheckpoint`, Trainer.cs:76). This is genuine gradient descent on a real model — the canonical from-scratch pre-training loop. But the code also does this: a whole-repository grep for `new Trainer(` (including the Tests folder, excluding only bin/obj) returns zero matches, so this class is never instantiated anywhere; the only cross-folder references to the `TrainingLoop` project are `using TrainingLoop.Optimizers;` (the AdamW/scheduler namespace, not the `Trainer` class) in `11.DPO\DpoTrainer.cs:8` and in two test files. So the repository's one complete general pre-training loop is dead code, and no Finding 1-27 ever named it. Reconciling the full census against Findings 20-26: those findings covered `DpoTrainer` (11.DPO), `IncrementalTrainer` (14.ContinuousLearning), `AutonomousTrainer` (16.AutonomousLearning), `DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer` (18.CapabilityTraining), `RoutingProfileTrainer` (26.TrainableRouter), and `OrchestrationReasoningTrainer` (43.SmartSkillsDomain) — every census hit except `04.TrainingLoop\Trainer.cs`, which is the single uncovered trainer. The code also surfaces four newly-named "adaptive" trainers in the smart-skills domain that no finding names individually: `OrchestrationReasoningAdaptiveTrainer` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\OrchestrationReasoningContracts.cs:304), `PromptToTaskAdaptiveTrainer` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\PromptToTaskContracts.cs:299), `ResultGapAdaptiveTrainer` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\SkillSelectionContracts.cs:1109), and `SkillSelectionCorrectionAdaptiveTrainer` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\SkillSelectionContracts.cs:1321). These are per-user "adaptive" trainers whose confidence is count-derived, not gradient-derived — for example `PromptToTaskAdaptiveTrainer.TrainUserLocal` sets `Confidence: Math.Min(1.0, positiveCount / 3.0)` at PromptToTaskContracts.cs:371 (a fraction of a small positive-example count), which matches Finding 23's already-established code fact that the whole `43.SmartSkillsDomain` folder contains zero gradient/optimizer/DpoTrainer code. So they are new SURFACES but the same counting mechanism, not a new capability. The three adapter hierarchies named as candidates in the task (`ResultGapLearningMachineAdapter` / `PromptToTaskLearningMachineAdapter` / `OrchestrationReasoningLearningMachineAdapter`, each carrying a `TrainBatchAsync` at ResultGapLogicalHierarchy.cs:29, PromptToTaskLogicalHierarchy.cs:33, OrchestrationReasoningLogicalHierarchy.cs:45) were already named by Finding 27 as "three smart-skills adapter hierarchies," so they are not fresh. Finally, the two folders flagged as unknowns are clear: a grep of `24.Compression` and of `14.MachineLearning` for `class *Trainer`, `TrainBatchAsync`, `gradient`, `optimizer`, and `DpoTrainer` returns zero in each — `14.MachineLearning` holds classic textbook primitives (a `MultilayerPerceptron` with an epoch loop, a `QLearning` table) that are not wired into the model/DPO story, and `24.Compression` holds compression families, so neither hides an uncovered trainer.

**(2) PROVENANCE-OF-ARTIFACTS — the pinned source commit IS in this repository, so the models trace to code here; only the metric snapshots were imported.** The repository fact is that `git merge-base --is-ancestor 2a1fab366b4c0cfab0f7f9a4473c04d667ca9ce2 HEAD` returns SUCCESS (exit code 0), and `git cat-file -t 2a1fab366b4c0cfab0f7f9a4473c04d667ca9ce2` returns `commit` — so the commit that the prompt-to-task checkpoint pins as its `sourceBaseCommit` is a real, present ancestor of the audited HEAD (0f221ac89) inside THIS repository. In plain terms: the published prompt-to-task model declares it was built from a commit that genuinely lives in this repo's own history, so the model is traceable to code here, not imported from an outside codebase. Separately, a document/data fact stands alongside it without contradicting it: the run summaries under `43.LearningLocalWikiData\artifacts\runner-metrics\` record a `resultRoot` under `C:\Users\lubao\Documents\...` (established in Finding 20), meaning the training RUNS executed in the user's personal Documents scratch area and the copies now under `43.LearningLocalWikiData\artifacts\` are curated imported snapshots. So the two facts are consistent: the CODE that produces the models is present and version-anchored in this repo (the `sourceBaseCommit` is an ancestor), while the specific metric/checkpoint ARTIFACT files are imported snapshots — reproducible in principle from the present code but not generated in place under the repo. Bottom line: published models are traceable to code in THIS repository; only the metric artifacts are imports. No new provenance gap.

**(3) CLAIM SWEEP — no documentation overclaim; the README is explicitly hedged and the CLAUDE.md lines are cautionary gates.** The document claims, at README.md:28, that the stack "contains a BPE tokenizer, a transformer, a training loop, a LoRA adapter, and a from-scratch DPO trainer"; at README.md:91 it groups `04.TrainingLoop`, `10.LoRA`, `11.DPO`, `14.ContinuousLearning`, and `26.TrainableRouter` under a "Training" heading; and at README.md:111 it calls the repo "the trainable core ... from-scratch models, DPO and LoRA training." Against that, the document itself supplies the honesty guard at README.md:32: "It is a study and reference stack, not a finished product. Some parts are trained and exercised for real; others are honest skeletons that establish an interface and a shape without a working model behind them. The project labels each component for what it is rather than for what it aspires to be." That hedge covers exactly the gaps Findings 25-26 found (that `26.TrainableRouter` is keyword counting and the `14.ContinuousLearning`/`16.AutonomousLearning` LoRA chain is a hollow pipe), the "Training" heading is a folder-bucket label expressly introduced as "a representative example from each group" (README.md around :88), and no README sentence asserts a working trained model where the code has none. The CLAUDE.md lines matching "trained"/"DPO"/"learns" are development GATES that demand more proof, not capability claims — for example the `CODE_IMPLEMENTATION_CLAIM_GATE` at CLAUDE.md:152 and the `REAL_TRAINER_ROUTE_B_GATE` at CLAUDE.md:252 both require independent evidence before a trainable claim may stand. So no documentation claim is STRONGER than what Findings 20-27 established; if anything the docs understate, consistent with Finding 27's "honest understatement." No new overclaim gap.

**(4) DEAD-SWITCH SWEEP — the supervised-pretraining config is a dead switchboard, led by an unread WeightDecay dial.** Clearest case: the code declares `public float WeightDecay { get; init; } = 0.1f;` at `04.TrainingLoop\TrainingConfig.cs:21` (a standard training dial that shrinks weights each step), but a whole-repo grep for `WeightDecay` finds only this declaration and a test method NAME (`Tests\AdamWTests.cs:52`, `Step_WeightDecay_ReducesWeightMagnitude`, which tests AdamW's own internal decay, not this field). The one loop that would apply it, `Trainer.cs`, calls `optimizer.Step(model.Parameters(), lr)` at Trainer.cs:68 with no weight-decay argument and never reads `_cfg.WeightDecay` — so this dial is declared and read by no code at all. Second, systemic case: the ENTIRE `TrainingConfig` record — `BatchSize` 8, `SeqLen` 2048, `TotalSteps` 10000, `MaxLr`, `MinLr`, `WarmupSteps`, `GradAccumSteps`, `GradClipNorm`, `CheckpointEvery`, `LogEvery` (TrainingConfig.cs:13-24) — is read only inside `Trainer.cs`, and `Trainer` is never instantiated anywhere (the zero-`new Trainer(` fact from Sweep 1). So the whole supervised-pretraining switchboard is dead: the dials exist, one real trainer reads them, and nothing ever constructs that trainer. Third, lesser case: `42.ProjectWikiDomain\ProjectWikiDomainModels.cs:695` declares a `LearningRate` field on the DPO-metrics record, and the only reader of `.LearningRate` in the repository is a test assertion at `Tests\ProjectWikiDomainTests.cs:1106` — no production path consumes it (it is carried into the serialized run summary as telemetry only). For contrast and honesty, a nearby look-alike is NOT dead: `42.ProjectWikiDomain\ProjectWikiRequestRoutingTrainableModel.cs:21` declares `private const double LearningRate = 0.14` and genuinely uses it at :74-79, so it was excluded.

**(5) CONTRADICTION CHECK — the five distinct manifest types are name-isolated, but ONE shared type is written to the same filename by two subsystems with a recursive-by-name loader.** The code fact is that the five name-alike manifest types Finding 3 listed write to DISTINCT filenames, so no two of the five ever describe the same file: `GeneratedCapabilityCheckpointManifest` -> `generated_capability_manifest.json` (constant at 18.CapabilityTraining\TeachingRegistryPersistence.cs:18), `SkillSelectionCheckpointManifest` -> `skill-selection-manifest.json` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\SkillSelectionMachine.cs:61), `PromptToTaskCheckpointManifest` and `OrchestrationReasoningCheckpointManifest` -> each writes its own `models\<name>\<name>-v1\manifest.json` (43.SmartSkillsDomain\XiigenExtensionApiService.cs:1408 and :1427), and DesignConversion's `CheckpointManifestFileName` -> `checkpoint-manifest.json` (44.DesignConversionDomain\DesignConversionRealS3EvidenceBundleValidator.cs:61). Those five are path-isolated. The real conflict lives INSIDE one of those types: `GeneratedCapabilityCheckpointManifest` is written to the identical filename `generated_capability_manifest.json` by TWO different subsystems that assume different things about it. Code fact A: `18.CapabilityTraining` writes it as its DPO-trained generated-capability checkpoint (`SaveGeneratedCapabilityManifest`, DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:319) and then reloads EVERY such file under its checkpoint root by a recursive filename scan — `Directory.GetFiles(_checkpointRoot, GeneratedCapabilityManifestFileName, SearchOption.AllDirectories)` at DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:586 and again at TeachingRegistryPersistence.cs:281 — building its replay/parent corpus and treating each file as a real DPO checkpoint whose `PolicyCheckpointPath` points to serialized model weights. Code fact B: `42.ProjectWikiDomain` emits the same-named file from thirteen `*ManifestPublisher.cs` classes; for example `ProjectWikiRequestRoutingManifestPublisher.cs:70` writes a file literally named `generated_capability_manifest.json`, but the `policy.bin` it references is a JSON snapshot of the training-result object (`kind = "project_wiki_request_routing_policy_state"`, ProjectWikiRequestRoutingManifestPublisher.cs:71-77), NOT a serialized transformer policy. So the two subsystems disagree about what a `generated_capability_manifest.json` file's checkpoint actually contains — real DPO weights versus a JSON snapshot of a result object. The conflict is latent rather than proven-live: `18.CapabilityTraining` uses a `_checkpointRoot` (defaulted when null, DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:44) while `42.ProjectWikiDomain` uses an `artifactRoot` supplied from request metadata (`artifact_root`, ProjectWikiTrainingFlowAdapter.cs:44); IF those two roots ever overlap on disk, 18's recursive-by-name loader would ingest 42's project-wiki routing manifests as generated-capability DPO checkpoints and try to read a JSON snapshot as model weights. Two subsystems, one manifest schema, one filename, and a recursive-by-name loader is a real cross-subsystem contradiction waiting on a shared root.

**Verdict:** fresh-gap hunt = THREE NEW GAPS, TWO SWEEPS CLEAN. New gaps: (1) an uncovered, complete, real gradient pre-training loop `04.TrainingLoop\Trainer.cs:24` that is dead code (zero `new Trainer(` anywhere, so no finding audited it and nothing runs it); (4) a dead training switchboard led by an unread `WeightDecay` dial (TrainingConfig.cs:21, zero readers) and, more broadly, the whole `TrainingConfig` read only by the never-instantiated `Trainer`; and (5) a shared `GeneratedCapabilityCheckpointManifest` written to the identical filename `generated_capability_manifest.json` by both `18.CapabilityTraining` (real DPO checkpoint, recursively loaded by name at DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:586) and `42.ProjectWikiDomain` (a JSON-snapshot policy, ProjectWikiRequestRoutingManifestPublisher.cs:70-77) — a latent cross-contamination conflict conditioned on the two roots overlapping. Clean sweeps: (2) provenance is sound — the pinned `sourceBaseCommit` 2a1fab366b4c0cfab0f7f9a4473c04d667ca9ce2 IS an ancestor of HEAD, so the models trace to code in THIS repo and only the metric snapshots are imports; and (3) documentation does not overclaim — the README explicitly hedges "honest skeletons ... without a working model behind them" (README.md:32) and CLAUDE.md's training lines are proof-demanding gates. Side note (not a gap): four newly-named user-local `*AdaptiveTrainer` classes (OrchestrationReasoningContracts.cs:304, PromptToTaskContracts.cs:299, SkillSelectionContracts.cs:1109 and :1321) are fresh surfaces but the same count-based mechanism Finding 23 already classified — no new capability.

_Cycle 3 (breadth: real training and DPO across all sibling subsystems) complete: Findings 20-28._

### Finding 29 — Gap map: the typed DPO legal chain (Cycle 4, Task 1)

**Plain-language framing.** The plan insists that a DPO training pair (DPO = Direct Preference Optimization, the training method that teaches a model to prefer a good answer over a bad one) may be produced ONLY by walking a fixed chain of named C# types, in order, with a legal gate in the middle. This finding builds a per-link gap map for the SEVEN links of that chain and, for each, keeps two things strictly apart: what a plan DOCUMENT requires (quoted with its `file:line`) and what the CODE actually contains today (quoted with its `file:line`). Glossary of the seven link names, each of which is a proposed type or token: (1) `GraphDpoSourceRow` is the raw "these two answers were compared" input row; (2) `GraphDpoPairCandidate` is a not-yet-legal candidate the builder emits with its train-ready flag forced false (`DpoTrainReady=false`); (3) `TriadLegalProjection` is the single three-way gate — raw comparison event + legal provenance envelope + training batch — that alone may promote a candidate, and its final go-decision token is `G_DPO_FINAL`; (4) `GraphDpoPair` is the canonical 14-field unit-level training row the projection mints; (5) `LearningTrainingItem` / JSONL is the tokenizable materialization of that row (JSONL = one JSON object per line); (6) the dataset load reads that JSONL back for training; (7) the one training step is the actual gradient update. Audited code: repository `C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation`, branch `DpoImplementation`, HEAD `0f221ac89`, working tree verified clean (`git status --porcelain` empty, `git rev-parse HEAD` = `0f221ac89cff57af4205d342ead51213425c0c45`) before and after; read/grep only, no build, no tests, no edits.

**Method note (reuse over re-audit).** Proven facts are carried forward by Finding number: Finding 5 (the five chain type names return zero code hits and `11.DPO` holds only ungated numeric preference math), Finding 20 (the project-wiki gradient DPO runs through a private pair-to-batch path that bypasses the typed chain), Finding 23 (`ResultGapTrainableDpoCandidate` is the nearest in-repo analogue of the not-ready candidate — staged-only, license/privacy fenced, never auto-promoted), Finding 27 (the graph machine's own `TrainBatchAsync` fail-closes), and Findings 6/15 (the FLOW-38 force-false / reject-true write discipline is the proven fail-closed fence pattern). Two fresh grep-level checks were run this task. Fresh check A: `LearningTrainingItem` is a REAL shipped carrier — the code defines the four-field record at `00.Core\LearningMachineContracts.cs:35-44` (fields `ItemId`, `Prompt`, `ChosenResponse`, `RejectedResponse`), and a repository-wide grep returns 38 references across 25 files, with live production consumers in `27.TranslationDomain\TranslationMachine.cs`, `43.SmartSkillsDomain\SkillSelectionMachine.cs`, and the design-conversion domain (`44.DesignConversionDomain\DesignConversionMachine.cs`, `...\DesignConversionTrainingPipeline.cs`, `...\DesignConversionDpoPolicyCheckpointBridge.cs`, plus `25.ModelServer\DesignConversion\DesignConversionDpoTrainingHandoff.cs`) — so the chain's tail carrier already exists and is exercised. Fresh check B: nothing converts any existing candidate/pair record into a `LearningTrainingItem` destined for the graph machine's training entry — a grep for `.TrainBatchAsync` call sites shows every non-test caller is inside the design-conversion domain building its own `LearningBatch<DesignDomain>`, while the only `DynamicGraphMachine.TrainBatchAsync` callers are four test sites (`Tests\DynamicGraphMachineHonestUntrainedTests.cs:41`, `:66`, `:88` and `Tests\DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactoryTests.cs:370`) and the only `LearningBatch<DynamicGraphDomain>` construction anywhere is in that honest-untrained test — so, as expected, zero production paths feed a graph pair into graph training. Fresh re-confirmation of Finding 5: standalone whole-word `GraphDpoPair` returns 0 code hits; the 9 raw `GraphDpoPair` substring hits are all the unrelated compound `CrossStackBusinessFlowGraphDpoPair` (record at `42.ProjectWikiDomain\ProjectWikiDomainModels.cs:923-931`), and `GraphDpoSourceRow`, `GraphDpoPairCandidate`, `TriadLegalProjection`, `G_DPO_FINAL` each return 0.

**How to read the table.** The columns keep code-fact and document-claim apart by construction: the "Plan requires" column cites a plan-document `file:line` (a claim on paper), and the "Exists today" column cites a code `file:line` (a fact in the repository). A link is BUILT only when a code anchor matches the named type; a link is a live carrier when the code anchor exists and is consumed but the typed chain does not yet flow into it.
