Because `LoraLinear.Forward` returns the same shape as `Linear.Forward`, the block's existing `Forward` would then automatically compute base + correction.
- OR add an adapter-aware forward path: give `CausalSelfAttention` (or `TransformerBlock`) an optional adapter map and call `adapter.Forward(x)` in place of `_qProj.Forward(x)` etc. when an adapter is present.

Either way, the hard-coded `training: true` in `LoraLinear.Forward` (10.LoRA/LoraLinear.cs:57) must be replaced by a real teach/answer flag so dropout is off when the model answers.

**(3) Pure code vs. the training boundary.** Writing the wiring above -- swapping the sub-layer, registering the adapter parameters, threading a teach/answer flag -- and unit-testing that the wiring changes the computed logits is PURE CODE work; no training run is needed to write or verify it. Proving that the wired adapters actually improve the model's answers (that DPO teaching now sticks) requires an actual DPO/LoRA training run, which is the DEFERRED training work. BOUNDARY -- TRAINING WORK, DEFERRED: this finding stops at the code-wiring diagnosis. STOP. I do NOT design, run, or verify any training behaviour here.

**(4) How a human confirms the fix later without running anything now.** After the glue is added, these assertions -- which FAIL today -- would PASS, and each needs only a single forward pass (no training):

- Build a model, call `LoraApplicator.Apply`, then manually set an adapter's `lora_b` to a non-zero tensor (bypassing training). Assert that `model.Forward(sameInput)` now yields DIFFERENT logits than the same model computed before the adapter existed (or with `lora_b` = 0). Today the logits are identical, which proves the adapter is ignored; after the fix they differ.
- Assert that `model.Parameters()` contains the adapter parameters (`lora_a`, `lora_b`) after `Apply`. Today they are absent, because the adapters are detached from the model.
- Assert that `LoraLinear.Forward` is deterministic in answer mode (a fixed input gives identical output across repeated calls). Today this cannot even be expressed, because dropout is hard-wired on.

**Verdict:** CONFIRMED and unresolved at HEAD 0f221ac89. The LoRA adapter factory (`LoraApplicator`) freezes the base and builds `LoraLinear` correction modules, and `LoraLinear.Forward` implements the base+correction math -- but nothing attaches those adapters to the model. The forward path (`LlmModel.Forward` -> `TransformerBlock.Forward:43` -> `CausalSelfAttention.Forward` using `_qProj/_kProj/_vProj/_oProj`) is hard-wired to the frozen projections, so the model's answers -- and DPO's scores and gradients, which run through the same `model.Forward` -- never include any learned correction. Both non-test callers (LlmIngredientLogicalUnits.cs:344 and TeachingRegistryPersistence.cs:322) build the adapters and then drop them (case c): one uses the list only for a count, the other discards it outright. The remaining work is pure code wiring (swap the attention projections for the adapter modules, register the adapter parameters, and turn dropout off at answer time); confirming that wiring needs only a single-forward-pass logit-difference test, whereas proving the teaching itself improves answers crosses into the deferred training runs and is out of scope here.

### Finding 37 — The from-scratch pre-training loop is finished code with no ignition key: nothing ever constructs it (Cycle 5, Task 2)

**Plain-language background.** Pre-training is the first and largest teaching stage of a language model: you take a mountain of raw text, cut it into batches, ask the half-formed model to predict the next word, measure how wrong it was (the "loss"), compute which direction each weight should move to be less wrong (the "gradients"), nudge the weights with an optimizer (the algorithm that turns gradients into weight updates), and every so often save a checkpoint (a snapshot of the model's weights to disk). The folder `04.TrainingLoop` contains a hand-written, from-scratch version of exactly this — the class `Trainer`. A prior audit cycle found that this class is dead: nothing anywhere ever writes `new Trainer(...)`, so the loop is real, finished machinery with no ignition key. This finding re-confirms the deadness at the current commit, describes what the loop already does correctly, inventories the parts a real starter would need (all of which already exist as usable code), corrects the earlier cycle's "dead weight-decay switch" claim with line evidence, and draws the line between the pure-code work that remains and the actual training run, which stays deferred.

**(1) The loop is real gradient code, and it is confirmed dead at this commit.** A whole-repo search for a caller returns nothing:

- `grep -rn "new Trainer(" --include=*.cs` (excluding `bin`, `obj`, and the stale `.claire`/`.clone` worktree copies) matches **zero** lines. No production file and no test file ever constructs a `Trainer`. The class `Trainer` (04.TrainingLoop/Trainer.cs:24) is therefore dead: fully written, never started.

What the dead loop already does correctly (it is genuine gradient code, not a stub):

- The constructor `Trainer(TrainingConfig cfg)` (Trainer.cs:30) builds a learning-rate schedule `CosineWithWarmup` (the plan for how fast the model learns over time — a warm-up ramp then a cosine decay) from the config (line 33).
- `TrainAsync(LlmModel model, AdamW optimizer, DataLoader loader, ...)` (Trainer.cs:36) is the loop. It creates the checkpoint folder (line 39), then runs `while (_globalStep < _cfg.TotalSteps)` (line 41).
- Inside each step it does gradient accumulation (adding up gradients over several small batches to imitate one big batch on limited memory): the inner loop `for acc = 0 .. GradAccumSteps` (line 47) pulls a batch with `loader.NextBatch()` (line 49), runs the forward pass `model.Forward(inputTensor)` to get the logits (the model's raw next-word scores, line 56), computes the cross-entropy loss (line 57), and back-propagates with a hand-written gradient `BackwardLoss(...)` (line 61).
- `BackwardLoss` (Trainer.cs:97-130) is real, correct math, not a placeholder: it computes the softmax of each row and sets the gradient to `softmax − one_hot` (lines 121-126) — the exact textbook derivative of cross-entropy with respect to the logits — then calls `logits.Backward(...)` (line 129) to push that gradient through the network.
- After accumulation it clips the gradient norm to stop exploding gradients (`TensorOps.ClipGradNorm`, line 65), asks the scheduler for the current learning rate (line 67), takes one optimizer step `optimizer.Step(model.Parameters(), lr)` (line 68), and zeroes the gradients (line 69).
- It honours DNA rule 8 (write the record before advancing): it saves the checkpoint `model.SaveCheckpoint(...)` (lines 75-76) **before** it increments `_globalStep++` (line 78).

So the loop is complete and coherent. The only thing wrong with it is that no one ever calls it.

**Inventory: every ingredient a real starter needs already exists as constructible code.** A working entry point for `TrainAsync` needs five things — a built model, a data loader, an optimizer, a filled config, and a scheduler. All five exist and compile today:

- **Built model — `LlmModel` (the from-scratch decoder-only transformer):** class at 03.TransformerModel/LlmModel.cs:17, constructor `LlmModel(ModelConfig cfg)` at line 28. It exposes the exact methods `Trainer` calls: `Forward` (line 47) and `SaveCheckpoint` (line 66); `Parameters()` and `ZeroGrad()` are inherited from its `Module` base class (proven in production by the sibling `DpoTrainer`, which calls both on an `LlmModel`). It is constructed all over the repo on production paths (e.g. 18.CapabilityTraining/DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:481, 42.ProjectWikiDomain/ProjectWikiDpoMetricRunner.cs:125, experiments-v2/.../Program.cs:55). **EXISTS, constructible.**
- **Data loader — `DataLoader` (streams training batches from disk):** class at 02.DatasetPipeline/DataLoader.cs:12, constructor `DataLoader(string dataDir, int batchSize, int seqLen)` at line 22, and `NextBatch()` at line 32 returns `(inputs, targets)` where the targets are the inputs shifted left by one token (line 43) — exactly what next-word prediction needs. The code EXISTS and is constructible, with two caveats: (a) a repo-wide `grep "new DataLoader("` matches **zero** lines — nothing ever constructs it either; and (b) it requires a directory that already contains tokenized `.npy` shards on disk and throws `InvalidOperationException` if the folder holds none (lines 27-28). So the loader is finished code that is (i) uncalled and (ii) waiting for data that a training run would have to place. **Code EXISTS; no caller; needs on-disk `.npy` data.**
- **Optimizer — `AdamW` (Adam with decoupled weight decay — the algorithm that turns gradients into weight updates):** class at 04.TrainingLoop/Optimizers/AdamW.cs:16, constructor at line 30, `Step` at line 40. Unlike the pre-training path, this one IS constructed on a production path — `11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs:32` builds `new AdamW(weightDecay: 0.01f)` — plus many tests. **EXISTS, constructible.**
- **Filled config — `TrainingConfig` (all hyperparameters in one settings object):** record at 04.TrainingLoop/TrainingConfig.cs:11 with every field defaulted (batch size, sequence length, learning rates, warm-up and total step counts, gradient-accumulation steps, clip norm, weight decay, checkpoint folder and interval, log interval — lines 13-24). A repo-wide `grep "new TrainingConfig("` matches **zero** lines, so it is never instantiated; but because every field has a default, `new TrainingConfig { ... }` is ready to use as-is. **EXISTS, ready.**
- **Scheduler — `CosineWithWarmup` (the learning-rate plan):** already constructed inside the `Trainer` constructor itself (Trainer.cs:33). **EXISTS.**

Conclusion of the inventory: **nothing is structurally missing.** All five parts are real, constructible code. What is missing is only the single glue file that news them up and calls `TrainAsync`, plus the on-disk tokenized data the loader would read.

**(2) The "dead weight-decay switch" from the earlier cycle, corrected with line evidence.** Weight decay is a penalty that gently shrinks weights on every step to discourage over-fitting. The earlier cycle flagged it as a dead switch. Read at the byte level, the truth is more precise, and it is worth stating honestly:

- **Inside the optimizer, weight decay is LIVE and read on every step — not ignored.** The field `_weightDecay` (AdamW.cs:20) is set from the constructor (line 35), and `Step` actually applies it at **AdamW.cs:73**: `w[i] *= 1f - lr * _weightDecay;` (the decoupled-weight-decay line that is the whole reason "AdamW" differs from plain "Adam"). A unit test already proves this works: `Step_WeightDecay_ReducesWeightMagnitude` at **Tests/AdamWTests.cs:52** starts a weight at 10 with a zero gradient and asserts it shrinks below 10 after one step (line 60). So the earlier "dead weight-decay switch" claim is **refuted at the optimizer-step level**: the math is present, correct, and tested.
- **The switch that IS dead sits one level up, at the config-to-optimizer wiring.** `TrainingConfig.WeightDecay` (TrainingConfig.cs:21, `= 0.1f`) is never handed to any `AdamW` constructor anywhere: a repo-wide `grep ".WeightDecay"` matches only its own definition (line 21) and a test method's name (AdamWTests.cs:52) — no code ever reads the config value. The one production optimizer that is built (`DpoTrainer.cs:32`) hard-codes `weightDecay: 0.01f` and ignores the config; the pre-training path builds no optimizer at all because `Trainer` is dead. So the config's `0.1` knob is inert — a real dead switch, but a wiring gap, not a broken calculation.
- **A genuinely dead constructor parameter, noted for honesty:** `AdamW`'s constructor takes a learning-rate argument `lr` (AdamW.cs:30, default `1e-3f`) that is **never stored** — there is no `_lr` field (the only fields are `_beta1`, `_beta2`, `_weightDecay`, `_eps`, lines 18-21). It is harmless because `Step` receives an explicit `lr` from the scheduler on each call, but a caller who writes `new AdamW(lr: 3e-4f)` and expects the optimizer to remember that rate would be silently wrong.

**What is missing to finish it — as pure code.** One new production (non-test) entry point that constructs the five ingredients and starts the loop. Concretely, it must:

1. build the config: `var cfg = new TrainingConfig { CheckpointDir = ..., TotalSteps = ..., BatchSize = ..., SeqLen = ..., ... };`
2. build the model: `var model = new LlmModel(modelConfig);`
3. build the optimizer **passing the config's weight decay** — `var optimizer = new AdamW(weightDecay: cfg.WeightDecay, ...);` — which simultaneously closes the dead config switch from section (2);
4. build the loader over a folder of tokenized shards: `var loader = new DataLoader(dataDir, cfg.BatchSize, cfg.SeqLen);`
5. start the loop: `await new Trainer(cfg).TrainAsync(model, optimizer, loader);`

This wiring is not speculative — the repo already contains a living twin that proves the pattern. `DpoTrainer` (11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs:28-36) constructs an `LlmModel`, builds an `AdamW` (line 32), sets a checkpoint directory, and drives a manual forward → hand-written backward → `optimizer.Step` → `ZeroGrad` loop (lines 38-62) — the same shape as `Trainer`, only for preference training instead of pre-training. The pre-training entry point is structurally simpler (one model, a `DataLoader` instead of a `DpoBatch`). The ignition pattern is therefore already validated in-repo; only the pre-training starter is absent.

**(3) Pure code vs. deferred training — the boundary.** Writing the entry point above, threading `cfg.WeightDecay` into the `AdamW` constructor, and adding a unit test that runs one loop iteration is **pure code work** — it needs no training run to write or verify. Actually **running** a pre-training run — producing and placing the tokenized `.npy` shards on disk, then executing `TrainAsync` for real steps to drive the loss down and emit checkpoints — is the **deferred training work**. BOUNDARY — TRAINING WORK, DEFERRED: this finding stops at the code-wiring diagnosis. STOP. I do NOT design, launch, or verify any training run here.

**(4) How a human confirms the fix later without running anything now.** After the entry point is added, these checks — all of which fail today — would pass, and none requires an actual training run:

- A repo-wide `grep "new Trainer("` finds a caller on a production (non-test) path. Today it finds nothing, which is the proof of deadness.
- A repo-wide `grep ".WeightDecay"` shows `cfg.WeightDecay` being read outside `TrainingConfig.cs` — i.e. passed into `new AdamW(weightDecay: cfg.WeightDecay, ...)` — proving the dead config switch is now plumbed. Today the only matches are the definition and a test name.
- The already-present unit test `Tests/AdamWTests.cs:52` confirms weight-decay correctness (a non-zero decay shrinks a weight) with a single optimizer step and no training run — so that half is confirmable today.
- A one-step smoke test can assert that after a single `TrainAsync` iteration at least one model parameter changed and a checkpoint file appeared in `cfg.CheckpointDir`. That exercises one forward, one backward, one optimizer step, and one save — enough to prove the ignition works, still short of a real training run.

### Finding 38 — The incremental trainer is a hollow pipe whose wiring promise points at a place that builds no model (Cycle 5, Task 3)

**Scope.** Read-only audit of `14.ContinuousLearning/Training/IncrementalTrainer.cs` — the "incremental trainer", the service that is supposed to run one small fine-tuning pass (one short round of extra training) over newly accumulated examples for a single domain and save the result as a LoRA adapter file. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation — a technique that freezes the big base model and trains only a tiny pair of extra matrices) is the mechanism it is meant to use. HEAD `0f221ac89cff57af4205d342ead51213425c0c45`, tree clean before and after. Cross-reference: Finding 36 (Task 1) already covers whether adapters are glued into the model's answer path; here I map only the trainer-side glue.

**(1) What exists NOW — a hollow pipe, quoted at the byte level.**

The whole engine is the method `TrainAsync` (14.ContinuousLearning/Training/IncrementalTrainer.cs:43-58). It does exactly three things and then lies:

- **It validates inputs.** Empty domain name → `OperationResult<string>.Fail("Domain cannot be empty.", "EMPTY_DOMAIN")` (lines 48-49; `OperationResult<string>` is this repo's success-or-failure return wrapper carrying either a value or an error code). No examples → `Fail("No training examples.", "EMPTY_EXAMPLES")` (lines 50-51).
- **It composes a file path but never creates the file.** `string path = Path.Combine(_adapterDir, $"{domain}.lora");` (line 55) builds a path like `adapters/CODE_Q.lora`. Nothing is written there.
- **It awaits nothing and returns the path as if training happened.** `await Task.CompletedTask;` (line 56 — `Task.CompletedTask` is an already-finished empty operation, so the `await` suspends on nothing and completes instantly), then `return OperationResult<string>.Ok(path);` (line 57 — reports success and hands back the path to a `.lora` file that does not exist).

There is no model, no gradient step (no weight update), no adapter, no write. The method's own comment admits it: "LoraApplicator.Apply and DpoTrainer.Step are wired up in 16.AutonomousLearning where a live LlmModel is available. Here we record the intended adapter path." (lines 53-54). The header comment (lines 11-21) spells out the four-step contract it is supposed to fulfil — Step 1 apply LoRA adapters, Step 2 a cross-entropy pass per training example, Step 3 a DPO preference step per DPO pair, Step 4 save adapter weights to `adapterDir/{domain}.lora` — and then defers all of it: "Wire up LoraApplicator and DpoTrainer in 16.AutonomousLearning.DataPipeline where a live TransformerModel.LlmModel is constructed." (lines 19-20). This is confirmed HOLLOW.

**(2) The wiring promise is currently FALSE — the place the comment points to builds no model.**

The comment names `16.AutonomousLearning.DataPipeline` as the home of the "live TransformerModel.LlmModel" (LlmModel = the from-scratch decoder-only transformer, the actual neural network). I falsified this two ways:

- **No model is constructed anywhere in 16.AutonomousLearning.** A grep for `LlmModel` across every non-test `.cs` file under `16.AutonomousLearning` returns **zero** lines. There is not even a `DataPipeline` folder — the directory holds `Core`, `DataSourceFinder`, `IdleDetector`, `Pipeline`, `Preprocessing`, `Training`, `TrustScorer` and nothing else. So the exact location the trainer's comment points at neither constructs a model nor exists under that name. The promise is, at this HEAD, plainly false.
- **The model IS constructible — just not where the comment claims.** `new LlmModel(config)` appears on real production paths elsewhere (18.CapabilityTraining/DefaultLogicalCapabilityTrainer.cs:481 and :510, 18.CapabilityTraining/TeachingRegistryPersistence.cs:311, 42.ProjectWikiDomain/ProjectWikiDpoMetricRunner.cs:125-126). So the raw material for step 1 exists; the incremental-training path simply never reaches for it.

**(3) How far the pretend-success travels downstream — it is recorded as a real, promoted, timestamped adapter.**

Two non-test callers consume the fake path, and neither ever checks that a file exists:

- **`AutonomousTrainer.TrainAsync`** (16.AutonomousLearning/Training/AutonomousTrainer.cs:49-79) — the replay-buffer wrapper (a replay buffer re-mixes old examples with new ones to avoid forgetting). It calls `await _trainer.TrainAsync(domain, mixedBatch, dpoPairs)` (line 66), and *because* the hollow trainer reports success, it treats the cycle as real: it adds every new example to the replay buffer "ONLY on successful training" (lines 72-75) and returns the fake path unchanged (line 78). So a no-op is what advances the buffer state.
- **`AutonomousLearningPipeline.RunAsync`** (16.AutonomousLearning/Pipeline/AutonomousLearningPipeline.cs:72-160) — the full 8-step nightly cycle. Step 5 calls the trainer (line 125) and takes `trainResult.Value!` — the fake `adapters/{domain}.lora` string. Step 6 wraps it in a record that asserts it is real: `new ModelAdapter(Domain: domain, AdapterPath: trainResult.Value!, TrainingExamples: weightedExamples.Count, TrainedAt: DateTimeOffset.UtcNow)` (lines 131-135) — a `ModelAdapter` (the record meaning "one trained LoRA adapter file", Core.cs:43-47) stamped with a real wall-clock "trained at" time and an example count, pointing at a file that was never written. It then passes a promotion gate (lines 142-145) and calls `_registry.Register(adapter)` (line 147), and finally returns a `CycleResult` (the cycle's report) whose `AdapterPath` is again the fake path (lines 155-159).
- **`ContinuousLearningPipeline.RunCycleAsync`** (14.ContinuousLearning/Pipeline/ContinuousLearningPipeline.cs) does the same one level down: on `trainResult.IsSuccess` it builds `new ModelAdapter(domain, trainResult.Value!, domainExamples.Count, DateTimeOffset.UtcNow)`, calls `_registry.Register(adapter)`, and returns it in the `newAdapters` list.

The registry does not defend against this. `DomainModelRegistry.Register` (14.ContinuousLearning/Registry/DomainModelRegistry.cs:33-39) only null-checks the record, then does `_registry[adapter.Domain] = adapter;` — no `File.Exists` on `AdapterPath`. So the pretend-success becomes the domain's officially registered, promoted adapter.

The one gate that *could* have caught it — the accuracy check `if (enhanced <= baseline)` (line 142), where accuracy comes from `IAccuracyEvaluator.Evaluate(domain)` — is inert against a missing file: there is **no production implementation of `IAccuracyEvaluator` at all** (the only implementer is a test double, `SequentialAccuracyAL` at Tests/AutonomousLearningTests.cs:590, which just dequeues scripted numbers like baseline-then-enhanced and never reads the adapter file). So in tests the fake path sails through promotion on invented numbers; in production the gate is not yet wired to anything. Net: the no-op success propagates all the way to a registered, timestamped "trained" adapter and a green CycleResult, with nothing in the chain ever confirming a real `.lora` file.

**(4) Remaining-work map — the trainer's own four-step contract, block by block.**

- **Step 1 — apply LoRA adapters to a live model. Building block EXISTS; glue missing.** `LoraApplicator.Apply(LlmModel model, LoraConfig config)` (10.LoRA/LoraApplicator.cs:17) freezes all base parameters and returns `IReadOnlyList<LoraLinear>` — the trainable adapter layers. The trainer already holds a ready config (`IncrementalTrainer.DefaultConfig`, rank 8 / alpha 16, IncrementalTrainer.cs:25-31). What is missing is the model itself: `IncrementalTrainer` has no `LlmModel` and never constructs one. Glue = construct or inject an `LlmModel`, then call `Apply`.
- **Step 2 — a cross-entropy pass per training example (cross-entropy = the standard next-word-prediction loss). Primitives EXIST; the loop must be written.** `LlmModel.Forward` and the autograd/optimizer machinery are real and proven in production by the sibling `DpoTrainer`, which runs forward → hand-written backward → `AdamW.Step` (AdamW = the Adam-with-weight-decay optimizer). But there is no ready-made "train these LoraLinear params on these TrainingExamples with cross-entropy" routine callable from 14. Glue = tokenize each example's prompt/response, forward, compute cross-entropy loss, backward, step the optimizer over the adapter parameters only.
- **Step 3 — a preference-training step per DPO pair (a DPO pair is a preferred-answer / rejected-answer example used to teach the model which answer is better). Building block EXISTS; glue missing.** `DpoTrainer.Step(DpoBatch batch, int step)` (11.DPO/DpoTrainer.cs:38) runs one preference step and returns `DpoMetrics`; it even saves policy checkpoints. But `IncrementalTrainer.TrainAsync` ignores its `dpoPairs` parameter entirely. Glue = construct `DpoTrainer(policy, reference, checkpointDir)`, convert the `IReadOnlyList<DpoPair>` into a `DpoBatch`, and call `Step`.
- **Step 4 — save adapter weights to the `.lora` file. Building block DOES NOT EXIST; must be built.** `10.LoRA` has only three files (LoraApplicator, LoraConfig, LoraLinear) and none can serialize. `LoraLinear`'s learned matrices are `private readonly Parameter _loraA` / `_loraB` (10.LoRA/LoraLinear.cs:18-19) with no public getter and no save method. A repo-wide search for any code that *writes* a `.lora` file returns exactly one hit — the comment at IncrementalTrainer.cs:17. A whole-model saver does exist (`LlmModel.SaveCheckpoint(path)`, 03.TransformerModel/LlmModel.cs:66, used by `DpoTrainer` to emit `.bin` policy checkpoints), but it writes the entire model, not a compact per-domain adapter. Glue = either add a `LoraLinear` weight serializer that writes `{domain}.lora`, or redefine the contract to persist via `SaveCheckpoint`.

Also to correct: the false header comment at lines 19-20 must be rewritten once the real wiring lands (or now, to stop pointing at a non-existent `16.AutonomousLearning.DataPipeline` that builds no model).

**(5) Boundary — code work vs. deferred training.**

CODE WORK (write it, no training run needed to author or unit-test it): construct/inject an `LlmModel`, call `LoraApplicator.Apply`, write the per-example cross-entropy loop, feed the DPO pairs to `DpoTrainer.Step`, build a `.lora` weight serializer and write the file, add a post-condition so `TrainAsync` cannot report success without a file on disk, and fix the misleading comment.

DEFERRED TRAINING (out of scope here): actually running the fine-tuning on real accumulated examples — driving the loss down over real steps and producing a genuinely improved adapter. BOUNDARY — TRAINING WORK, DEFERRED. This finding stops at the code-wiring diagnosis. STOP. I do not launch or verify any training run.

**How a human confirms the fix later without running anything now.**

- `TrainAsync` can no longer return `Ok` without a file existing at the returned path — after the fix it must `File.Exists(path)`-check (or write-then-verify) and return `Fail` otherwise. A unit test asserts the `.lora` file is actually written to disk and is non-empty after a one-example call. Both are checkable without a real training run (one forward / one step / one save is enough).
- A grep for `LlmModel` under `16.AutonomousLearning` (or wherever the trainer is wired) now finds a real construction — today it finds zero, which is the proof of the broken promise.
- The `dpoPairs` parameter is now read: a grep shows it converted into a `DpoBatch` and passed to `DpoTrainer.Step` — today it is silently discarded.
- The header comment at IncrementalTrainer.cs:19-20 no longer points at a non-existent `DataPipeline`.
- Downstream honesty is restored transitively: because `DomainModelRegistry.Register` and both pipelines trust the trainer's success, a `TrainAsync` that cannot succeed without a real file means a registered `ModelAdapter` now always corresponds to a real adapter on disk — no separate registry change required to stop the fake-adapter propagation.

### Finding 39 — The decorative graph slice: the exact production socket it was never plugged into (Cycle 5, Task 4)

**Scope and inheritance.** Read-only audit at HEAD `0f221ac89cff57af4205d342ead51213425c0c45`, audited tree clean before and after. This finding does NOT re-open two earlier verdicts and builds on them: Finding 4 established that the top-level `DynamicGraphMachine` (the "L5 learning machine" — the whole self-improving graph engine's outermost object) is an honest fail-closed scaffold, where every train/evaluate/export/import entry deliberately returns a `...NOT_ACTIVATED` refusal (the machine's honest "this is not switched on yet" answer). Finding 19 established that the fan-out chain (the machinery where one task spawns several parallel subtasks across graph levels and then merges them back) and ten of the eleven "measurement doors" (a measurement door = a mandatory contract gate that every quality number must pass through before it counts) are finished, test-covered code that is decorative in production: no live code path calls them. My job here is only the missing-wiring map — the exact production socket the slice was never plugged into, and, per door, whether plugging it in is plain code work or is legitimately blocked until training runs exist.

**(1) What exists NOW — the three assembled parts, with file:line.**

**Part A — the composition factory (the one place that assembles the graph machine).** `DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactory.CreateHonestUntrained` (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactory.cs:39) is the single assembler. In plain words it needs five things and does one job:

- `ISessionStorage storage` — the session-storage boundary where a run's state lives.
- `DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorBootstrapProfile profile` — the bootstrap profile: the graph's declared node definitions (the per-sub-phase producers, called "L2 logical units"), its edge bindings (the source-to-target relations, each naming a would-be learned authority), the tenant/domain/context identity, and a run budget.
- an optional `ProjectWikiTrainingFlowAdapter` — the hook into the project-wiki training flow, allowed to be null.
- internally, a composition function (`DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorComposition.Create`) that builds the actual runtime (its assistant plus its topology source).
- The one job: compose exactly once, then admit the result only if it is provably "honest untrained" — every node must carry no trained checkpoint, no "deterministic final authority" flag, no runtime quality claim, and only the baseline-candidate-generator role (the check `IsHonestUntrained`, lines 257-266); every edge must not claim final authority (lines 198-199, 243-244). If all that holds it retains one orchestrator identity and wraps it in `new DynamicGraphMachine(...)` (lines 128-129).

Confirmed dead in production: a whole-repo search (`DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactory` and `CreateHonestUntrained`, excluding `bin`, `obj`, and the `.claire`/`.clone` worktree copies) matches exactly two files — the factory itself and `Tests/DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactoryTests.cs`. No production caller. Equivalently, `new DynamicGraphMachine(` appears at exactly one place in the whole tree — inside this factory (line 128) — so the honest fail-closed machine of Finding 4 is never even constructed in a live process; its `...NOT_ACTIVATED` refusals cannot be reached by any running server because nothing running builds the object that would emit them.

**Part B — the fan-out chain and its production-facing entry.** The fan-out relation is a first-class, closed enum value: `GraphEdgeKind.FanOut` — "FanOut sends one source result to one or more target nodes" (00.Core/DynamicGraphContracts.cs:73), with its deterministic N-to-one merge-back defined by `IEdgeFanInMerger` (same file, line 417). The live-facing doorway into that chain is two public methods on the orchestrator assistant: `LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.RunFactorySelectedTargetExecutionAsync` (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:471) and `RunFactoryAdmittedSelectedTargetExecutionAsync` (:505). Both build and run `DynamicGraphFactorySelectedTargetExecutionComposition.Create` (:480 and :512), which wraps the edge-authority execution probe (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphFactorySelectedTargetExecutionComposition.cs:17) over the factory-aware selected-target executor (:69). Confirmed dead: a whole-repo search for `RunFactorySelectedTargetExecutionAsync`, `RunFactoryAdmittedSelectedTargetExecutionAsync`, and `DynamicGraphFactorySelectedTargetExecutionComposition` finds them referenced only by the assistant that defines them, their own composition file, one executor-adapter inside the same slice, and `Tests/`. No live request path calls either method.

**Part C — the eleven measurement doors, all behind one coordinator.** Every quality number in the slice is forced through `DynamicGraphMeasurementCoordinator` (21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/DynamicGraphMeasurementCoordinator.cs), whose own summary says it "composes target admission with numeric measurement while preserving each boundary's errors" (line 11). It exposes exactly eleven public methods — these are the eleven doors — each of which first admits a measurement target and then calls the matching meter class in 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical/DynamicGraph*Meter.cs:

1. `BindL2UntrainedHonesty` (:78) — untrained-honesty door.
2. `MeasureAblation` (:214) — ablation door (accuracy lost when a part is switched off).
3. `MeasureAccuracy` (:244) — held-out accuracy door.
4. `MeasureNovelty` (:272) — novelty door (how much is genuinely new vs already seen).
5. `MeasureRunSpeed` (:301) — run-speed door (wall-clock timing of a completed run).
6. `MeasureTrainingSpeed` (:322) — training-speed door (cost of one training batch).
7. `MeasureEdgeAuthorityControl` (:350) — edge-authority-control door (order + identity invariance).
8. `MeasureEdgeQualityEvidence` (:391) — composite edge-quality door (authority-control + novelty + ablation).
9. `MeasureEdgeAuthoritySelectionQualityEvidence` (:436) — atomic edge-authority-selection-quality door.
10. `MeasureEdgeIdentityQuality` (:489) — edge-identity door (answer unchanged when a decoy identity is swapped in).
11. `MeasureEdgeOrderQuality` (:526) — edge-order door (answer unchanged when candidate order is permuted).

Confirmed dead: a whole-repo search for `DynamicGraphMeasurementCoordinator.` (a call into any door) matches only `Tests/` and one standalone developer tool (`tools/DynamicGraphCompositionLiveLocalServer`). Widening the net to every meter class, both execution probes, and the coordinator, the ONLY files that reference them outside `Tests/` and `tools/` are the meters, the coordinator, and the two probes referencing each other — a closed island inside 21.AgentInfrastructure/Logical plus the one composition file in 15.OrchestratorModel. No production runtime, no server, no routing unit, and no business domain reaches in. So at this HEAD all eleven doors have zero live callers; the slice measures nothing in production.

**(2) Exactly what wiring is missing, and at which seam.** The live product's actual request path never touches any of Parts A/B/C. The only live server entry is the design-conversion model server — `25.ModelServer/DesignConversion/DesignConversionModelServerApi.cs` and its handlers — which routes an incoming job into the design-conversion domain (44.DesignConversionDomain) through an improvement-loop coordinator (`coordinator.RunAsync`, 44.DesignConversionDomain/DesignConversionThinClientJobRuntime.cs:90). That coordinator is a design-conversion object, not the graph. A type-level search proves the disconnect: `LogicalOrchestratorAssistant` is referenced ZERO times anywhere under `25.ModelServer` or `44.DesignConversionDomain`. And the assistant itself is only ever constructed by `DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorRuntime.CreateDefault` (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/DynamicGraphLogicalOrchestratorRuntime.cs:242+, reachable only through the dead factory of Part A) and by `TeachingSessionBuilder` (15.OrchestratorModel/Logical/TeachingSessionBuilder.cs, whose only callers are `Tests/`). No live host builds the assistant at all.

The concrete seams where a production caller would have to be inserted are therefore three, in order:

- **Seam 1 — construction.** A live host must build the honest-untrained machine by calling `DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactory.CreateHonestUntrained(storage, profile, adapter)` (DynamicGraphMachineCompositionFactory.cs:39), OR build the assistant directly through `LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.CreateDefaultCore` (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:137/149) while passing a real dynamic-graph node-output source and topology source. Those two injection points are the switch: in the default `CreateDefault` path both are left null (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:126, and the two `AdaptiveLogicalOrchestrator` constructions at :422-442), so even if the assistant were alive its dynamic-graph branch would stay inert.
- **Seam 2 — per-request execution.** On each real request the host must call `LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.RunFactorySelectedTargetExecutionAsync` (LogicalOrchestratorAssistant.cs:471). That is the only production-facing method that drives the fan-out chain into the edge-authority probe, and thence into door 9 (`MeasureEdgeAuthoritySelectionQualityEvidence`). Note the assistant's genuinely-live method `RunAsync` (:530) deliberately routes through the legacy/adaptive orchestrator and never calls the RunFactory* pair — so wiring here is not a one-line redirect; it is a new call site.
- **Seam 3 — the host that must own seams 1 and 2.** Today that is nowhere. The design-conversion model server (`DesignConversionModelServerApi.cs`) is the only live entry and it bypasses the assistant entirely, so the missing production caller must be added either inside that server's request path or in a new general orchestration endpoint that constructs the machine once and runs the chain per request.

**(3) Per item: pure code work vs blocked behind deferred training.** Wiring seams 1-3 so the chain and the two runnable doors fire is pure code work. Of the eleven doors, two can be exercised by simply running a real request through an untrained baseline node — no training needed — and nine cannot, because the number they measure only exists once a training run has produced a trained subject, a held-out split, or a learned edge authority.

- **PURE CODE WORK (2 doors):**
  - Door 1 — untrained-honesty (`BindL2UntrainedHonesty`, :78). This door measures the ABSENCE of training: it confirms the runtime reported "not ready", carries no trained-state evidence, performed no semantic classification, and matched no forbidden shortcut. It is precisely the door that would make the Finding 4 fail-closed scaffold's honesty machine-checkable at runtime. Its natural caller is the L2 node-execution path at the moment an untrained baseline generator runs — reachable the instant seams 1-2 are wired, with no training run required.
  - Door 5 — run-speed (`MeasureRunSpeed`, :301). Pure wall-clock timing (inference elapsed, throughput, routing elapsed) of an already-completed run. Its natural caller is the fan-out execution path emitting timings after a real request. No training needed.

- **BLOCKED BEHIND DEFERRED TRAINING (9 doors)** — each needs an artifact that only a training run creates, so its natural caller is part of the not-yet-started training loop:
  - Door 3 — accuracy (`MeasureAccuracy`, :244): consumes a before/after held-out evaluation pair; "before/after" is a training outcome by definition.
  - Door 4 — novelty (`MeasureNovelty`, :272): needs a train-vs-held-out split, which the training loop produces.
  - Door 2 — ablation (`MeasureAblation`, :214): compares a trained subject against its ablated counterpart; needs the trained subject.
  - Door 6 — training-speed (`MeasureTrainingSpeed`, :322): measures the cost of one completed training batch (`LearningBatchResult`); it IS a training-loop reading, and admits only an L5 target.
  - Door 11 — edge-order (`MeasureEdgeOrderQuality`, :526): permutation-invariance of a LEARNED edge authority; no learned authority exists without training.
  - Door 10 — edge-identity (`MeasureEdgeIdentityQuality`, :489): identity-control invariance of a learned edge authority; same blocker.
  - Door 7 — edge-authority-control (`MeasureEdgeAuthorityControl`, :350): the order+identity composite over a learned authority; same blocker.
  - Door 9 — edge-authority-selection-quality (`MeasureEdgeAuthoritySelectionQualityEvidence`, :436): authority-control plus novelty for the chosen edge; this is the door the fan-out chain would fire, but its novelty half needs a held-out split from training.
  - Door 8 — edge-quality-evidence (`MeasureEdgeQualityEvidence`, :391): the full composite (authority-control + novelty + ablation); blocked by all of the above.

BOUNDARY — TRAINING WORK, DEFERRED. Building the entry point that constructs the machine and runs the chain, and letting doors 1 and 5 fire, is authorable and unit-testable now. Producing the trained subjects, held-out splits, and learned edge authorities that doors 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, and 11 need is the deferred training work. This finding stops at the wiring diagnosis. STOP. I do not design, launch, or verify any training run.

**(4) How a human confirms the fix later without running anything.** Every check below fails today, and none needs an actual run:

- A whole-repo search for `CreateHonestUntrained` finds a caller on a production (non-test) path — today it matches only the factory itself and one test file.
- `new DynamicGraphMachine(` becomes reachable from a live host — today it exists only inside the factory that nothing live calls, so the honest fail-closed machine is never even built in production.
- `RunFactorySelectedTargetExecutionAsync` (or the `Admitted` variant) gains a caller outside `Tests/` — today only tests call it.
- `DynamicGraphMeasurementCoordinator.` gains a caller outside `Tests/` and `tools/`; specifically the two pure-code doors `BindL2UntrainedHonesty` and `MeasureRunSpeed` are reached from a live request path, proving the untrained-honesty and run-speed measurements now fire on real traffic.
- `LogicalOrchestratorAssistant` appears at least once under `25.ModelServer` or wherever the live host lives — today that type is referenced zero times there, which is the proof the graph slice is unplugged from the live server.

### Finding 40 — Full inventory: deterministic algorithms sitting where learned mechanisms are required (Cycle 5, Task 5)

**What this consolidates, in plain words.** Findings 20-28 pointed at individual places where a fixed hand-written algorithm (a keyword counter, a table of hand-picked weights, a rule list, a nearest-example lookup) sits where the plan asks for a LEARNED mechanism — one whose behaviour comes from training on examples. This finding is the complete table. For each place it records: what the code literally does (file:line), whether the NAME is honest or a mask (a keyword counter inside a project named "TrainableRouter" is a mask; a class named "Deterministic...Baseline" is honest), whether that code is the FINAL decision authority on a real product path (the code whose output actually decides, with no later override) or merely a fallback/baseline, and the remaining work — split into code plumbing (in scope: routing a decision through an already-built artifact, or removing a mask name) versus deferred training (out of scope here: training a model on pairs that already exist). Audited repo: C:\Projects\llm_mvp_core-DpoImplementation, branch DpoImplementation, HEAD 0f221ac89cff57af4205d342ead51213425c0c45 (git rev-parse verified before and after; git status --porcelain empty/clean before and after); read and grep only, no build, no tests, no edits. Greps excluded bin, obj, .claire\worktrees, .clone\worktrees; Tests\ treated as test-only.

**Glossary used below.** "Perceptron" = a simple trainable linear classifier whose per-word weights are nudged by each training example. "Nearest-neighbour lookup" = answering a query by finding the closest memorized example and returning its stored answer, instead of computing an answer from a trained model. "Edge authority" = the final say over which downstream unit a graph fork routes to. "Final authority" = the code whose output is the decision, with nothing learned overriding it afterwards. "Held-out gate" = a check that a trained thing scored well on examples it was not trained on.
